PRISONER'S SUICIDE. - Tragedy in a Warrington Police Cell

The circumstances attending the suicide of Robert Leatherbarrow (63), 5, Howard's Place, were inquired into by Mr. S. Brighouse and a jury at the ‘Varrington Coroner's Court on Tuesday. It appears that at noon on Monday Leatherbarrow was arrested and detained at the Police Station on a charge of begging. He was locked up in a cell but pleading that he was suffering from a mild attack of diarrhtea he was allowed the run of the lavatory, corridor and exercise yard. Nothing ,untoward occurred , during the afternoon, but when in the evening P.C. Howard visited the cells with the prisoners' teas he found Leatherbarrow ii the lavatory, hanging, by the chain of the flushing tank. The chain was wrapped round his neck but was not tied in any way. He was at once taken down but life was extinct. Mr. Brighouse inquired carefully as to the cause of the arrest. The deceased was well known to the police. Why should he not have been taken horse instead of being arrested? He could understand a tramp or "Weary Willie" being locked up for begging. Superintendent Monaghan explained that Leather- barrow had been many times cautioned about begging and had been spoken to no later than Monday morning. Giving evidence as the cause of death—suffocation by ,hanging—Dr. Smith said he knew the deceased well. He had been a great nuisance because of his begging and people had complained. Mrs. Leatherbarrow explained that the deceased was a tanner but was not working, and had been greatly depressed. He had no occasion to beg as he had a good home and everything he wanted. Mr. Brighouse said he hoped it would not be thought that the police bad done anything which was not right. The deceased had apparently made himself a nuisance sod the police were therefore perfectly justified in taking him into custody. There was n great deal of absurd nonsense talked about the liberty of the subject, but the subject had no right to liberty if he was a nuisance or danger to the community at large. He thought the .police had done everything which could be expected of them.

A verdict of Suicide whilst of unsound mind "was returned.

THE LAND LAWS.-TWO MEN FINED FOR TRESPASS.

Ernest Marsh and William Pennington were summoned at Newton Sessions on Thursday last week for a game trespass on Mr. Collier's farm, Newton Common.—lr. Arthur Browne, prosecuting for Lord Newton, said these :two young men were seen by P.C. Simpson on a bank side with a dog, which they were helping to get into a rabbit burrow. Be fetched the farmer's son, and they together beard one of. the defendants say they had just missed a bonny rabbit.—P.C. Simpson gave evidence to that effect, which John James Collier confirmed.— Defendants said they were ratting—not rabbiting.—Lord Newton's beadkeeper said there was plenty of game About there. The defendants had no business there.— Fined 2s. 6d. and costs each.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

Pioneers of the Red Cross Nurses.—The Queen's reference to Miss Florence Nightingale, in welcoming, the delegates to the International Red Cross Confer- ence recalls the fact that there is at least one other of the pioneer nurses of the Red Cross movement still amongst the living. This is a !tliss Doyle, who came from Carlow, in Ireland, and wont to Scutari under the charge of Miss Stanley. Miss Doyle is a Sister of Mercy, and is eighty-seven, or a little younger than Florence Nightingale. Ten veers ago she was received at Windsor Castle by Queen Victoria, who gave her the Royal Red Cross badge. The Seven Leading flostesses.—Mrs. George Corn- wallis West, the Lady Randolph Churchill, of former days, is a well-known writer on social topics, and has related for the benefit of American readers, some of her impressions of London Society. Few women could write with greater authority .on the subject, and among other details, Mrs. George Cornwallis West names the seven ladies whom she considers the most successful London hostesses. These are the Duchess of Devon- shire, the Duchess of Sutherland, the Duchess of Port- land, the Marchioness of Lansdowne, the Marchioness of Londonderry, the Countess of Jersey, and the Countess de Grey. Obviously Mrs. George Cornwallis West does not look for her hest hostesses amongst those who had only the butterfly existence of fashionable life. Those she names are all clever cultured women, who read and think, and possess an immense fund of general information. All are frequent entertainers of Royalty, and among the early country engagements of the King and Queen, is the proposed visit of their majesties to Lord ad Lady Lansdowne at Bowood this summer.

The Value of Pins.—One of the London fashion papers has been reminding us that the best dressed women rely much more upon pins than fastenings when getting into an alaborate gown. Undoubtedly they do, but then the majority of these fortunate individuals have a maid in attendance who thoroughly understands the art of "fixing up "her mistress, and making a success of the task. Those among us who have to dress and wait epon ourselves would do better to rely upon hooks and eyes, and buttons, and so make sure of a neat toilette though perhaps the effect may not be quite so good as it would have been had pins been skilfully employed. The Fashionable Fichu.—The rage for the fichu is tremendous and we may therefore reasonably conclude that it will not be long before leading dressmakers dis- card the idea. For wearing with very delicate dressy gowns the modish fichu is being built entirely of lace and chiffon, draped in becoming folds about the shoulders, the long ends being ornamented with soft ruches of the chiffon, and falls of the lace. The fichu, generally speaking is most becoming,and the individual bent upon bringing a last summer's frock up-to-date, should seize upon the notion. Pique Again.—Pique has put in an appearance again but the new toilettes are so trimmed as to render it inipossible for us to use the term " washing gowns "in connection with them. Silk, satin, taffeta, and braid in black or colours, are laid on, in the form of pipings and folds, and there is no question aboet the effect being exceptionally smart, but the woman with a small dress allowance will find a good linen so ornamented as to allow her making use of the home tub, and the home ironing-board the wiser choice. Transferring Braiding Patterns to Velvet.—When requiring to braid velvet, or any other material that cannot be stamped in the ordinary way, an excellent plan is to pin down the design to the fabric, and then prick the pattern over with a coarse pin. Fine powder should then be dusted over it, and when the paper is removed little dots of the powder will be found. By dabbing these dots with a tiny pad of cotton wool, they can be made to form a continuous line, which can be easily followed by the braider. A Hint for the Needlewoman.—Rhen working upon coarse linens, and cotton, the needle is very apt to run stiffly, and the cotton to break. The best procedure in such cases is to damp a piece of soap and rub it lightly alone the seam, when the difficulties described above will be found to have very nearly disappeared. Hair Dyes and Transformations.—lf the colour of our hair does not satisfy us, there is little doubt that it is better to rely< upon transformations than hair dyes, especially when the latter is applied by unskilled bands. In such cases, strange and undesirable hues all too often appear, and also lines and ridges where the last applica- tion has been allowed to flow too freely over the earlier one. And in choosing transformations, there is one point we should never overlook, and that is to select them in accordance with our age, remembering always that it is far better for the hair to be in advance of the, face, than the face to be in advance of the hair. So soon as the little tell-tale lines about the face and mouth begin to appear, a slight mingling of hair, just on the verge of losing its colour, should be added, and added again as time goes on, thereby rendering the " applied " hair much more difficult of detection, and very much more becoming than when the youthful aid all-over-the-same shades are strictly adhered to. The Medicine Habit.—There is a great number of people who find life's chief interest in taking medicine andindulging in all sorts of" cures," most of which are the merest quackery. To them Sir Frederick Treves indictment of the medicine habit will be as impalatable as it will be welcome to the inmates of the nursery,who will certainly not be backward in agreeing that all physic should be thrown to the dohs. But of course Sir Frederick Treves did not say or mean as much as has been assumed from the brief summary of his speech. What he did mean was, that health depended far more upon hygienic habits and proper sanitation' than in taking physic, and this stops short a long way from suggesting that all medicine is worthless. That this is the case with nine-tenths of what is swallowed by the public, is beyond all question, but then there are so many people, especially women, who are never happy unless taking a " tonic " or some nostrum or other, and it is to be feared they will be slow to act upon the advice to give up the medince habit. I The Women's Municipal Charter.—The Bill to enable women to sit as aldermen and councillors on County and Borough Councils, has revived all the usual argu- ments for and against women serving on public bodies., It is regarded as likely to promote the suffragist move- ment, and some even see in it the paving of the way to women sitting in Parliament and upon the Bench at Assizes. It does not seem likely that the Bill will be passed this Session in spite of the Government's inten- tions to this effect,but if it does it is not likely to lead to any of those results. Women are already eligible for election on boards of guardians, pariah councils and school boards, and if the ratepayera do not want women I on the larger Councils they would have simply to say I so, at the polling booths. In all probability it would be ' found that women are more disposed to be careful of the ratepayers; interests than men. In any case there is nothing very convincing in all the objections urged against their serving upon local public bodies, which, in many cases are not so perfect as to be impermeable of improvement. MADGE.

NEW COIFFURE ORNABIENTE.

Tassels have even found their way into the realm of hair ornaments, and the newest idea, says a London paper, is to twist a thick rope of gold cord among the coils of the coiffure finished with a heavy tassel falling over the right ear, and depending almost to the shoulders. Another very popular hair decoration consists af chaplets of mottled laurel leaves laid one over the other with a cluster of realistic-looking cherries de- ?xinding from them on either side, and although in some cases flowers are substituted for the cherries, there is no denying that fruit in the realm of hair ornaments is considered to give far more cachet to the toilet. Japanese medlars with a chaplet of shaded blue velvet leaves or unripe plums supplemented with delicate silver foliage are very much appreciated in this connec- tion, these decorations being worn with almost any description of evening toilet without refer- ence to style or material.

ZEPHYR SUMMER FROCK.

Charmingly dainty and pretty is the little washing frock which forms the subject of our sketch. The Material in which it is achieved is a zephyr of good quality, patterned with a scarlet stripe upon a white ground. The bodice is made in pinafore style, and is cut on the cross, with a seam down the middle of the front, at which the stripes meet in a point. Over the shoulders come three wide tucks, which are shaped in slightly to- wards the waist. Round the top of the bodice,

THE EGG-SHELL AS GAS MANTLE.

A gas mantle has recently been patentee in Germany in which the mantle itself con- sists of nothing more than egg-shell, this being, according .to the patent specification, particularly well suited for acetylene gas burners. The egg-shell, says the Engineer, is converted into a glowing state by means of the combustion of the gas inside, and is said to spread a pleasing and agreeable light.

A DAILY CONSTITUTIONAL,

There is hardly an instance of a long-lived man who has not been for the best part of his life a brisk walker, and for some reason or other has had to take exercise pretty well every day. Riding is all very well, and so are other exer- cises, but there is nothing like a good walk, de- clares the Queen, because it stimulates the blood and the muscles, and necessitates being in the open air. If those who complain of being stout would only think of this, and never omit a daily constitutional, they would be amply rewarded. It will keep them young, and their figures pre- sentable. It is so simple a remedy that no one heeds it; sitting about in the open air is all very well, and is far better than sitting in the house, but it does not keep you in good health. People who are bent on entering into the dissipations of the London season find it difficult to fit this walk in, but it is of the utmost importance that they should. It is all the more necessary when the late hours have been spent in hot stuffy rooms. It is quite another thing to over fatigue one- self. There is nothing better than to get into a healthy perspiration by walking. It is just like drinking a glass of cold water in the morning: it is so simple no one believes in it. This may not suit everybody, but those it does suit it will keep in health.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

BRAISED NECK OF VAL. —A neck of veal is made delicious .by braising, as follows: Take a neck of veal, two carrots sliced, one onion stuck with two cloves, one head of celery cut into halves, a bunch of mixed herbs, loz. of drip- ping, stock, seasoning to taste.. Trim the veal neatly, and wash and prepare the vegetables. Put the dripping into a braising-pan. When hot put in the vegetables and herbs. Put the on the top of these, and season with a little idt and pepper. Cook for about, fifteen minutes, _Luning the veal several times, them add enough stook just to cover the veal, and braise very slowlv for three hours. Take out the veal when tender, strain the stock, fre• it trom grease, and boil It down until it is reduced to half. Put the veal in the oven to brown, basting it% with the reduced stock for about fifteen minutes. Send up to table garnished with I reithiy -bailed Ming peak

FEAR IN CHILDREN.

An examination of 1,500 children, made in America, shews that thunderstorms are by far the worst terror of childhood. After- wards, in the order set down, come snakes, strangers, darkness, fire, death, disease, wild animals, policemen, water, insects, and ghosts. In the fear of rats and mice girls exceed boys in the ratio of 75 to 16. In boys fear begins to diminish after the fifteenth year, in girls after the eighteenth.

A GAUGE OF AN ARTESIAN WELL. To calculate the rate of flow of an arte- sian well, says Technical Literature, a simple plan is to lower a bottle of aniline fluid to a depth of, say, 500 ft., and then elec- trically explode a cap to burst the bottle. The ,time required for the fluid to appear at the surface gives an accurate gauge as to the velocity of the flow. It is claimed that this method gives results as accurate as a weir. The diameter of tile pipe being known, the rate of flow readily follows.

PREHISTORIC CONSUMPTIVE FOUND. From the examination of a skeleton dis- covered at Heidelberg, in Germany, which experts inform us belongs to the stone age, Professor Barteis has been able to discover that consumption was in existence at that distant period. He found characteristic signs of the disease, among them the decay of the fourth and fifth dorsal vertebrae (bones of the spinal column), and their conjunction, in an osseous (bony) formation with the sixtht, a sure sign of the disease. Then he dis- covered a deviation 'of the spinal column, caused by the illness, another sign, which left the professor no doubt about the truth of his discovery.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
GLASS GRAVESTONES.

A company of glass-workers has discovered that ordinary plate-glass will make a more durable monument than the hardest marble or granite known to stonemasons. Glass is practically indestructible. Wind, rain, heat, and cold have their effect on the hardest rock; solid granite eventually crumbles away, and one can seldom read the inscrip- tion on a stone gravestone fifty years old. A glass stone will look as fresh a couple of cen- turies after its manufacture as on the day it was erected, and the inscription can be made ineffaceable. The thick plate-glass used to glaze the port-holes of steamers can resist the stormiest sea, and is practically unbreakable.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
WITCHES' BROOMS ON TREES.

Among the objects which the leafless sea- son of the year makes conspicuous in our woods and hedgerows are certain dark, al- most globular agglomerations of small twigs on the boughs of many different kinds of trees, which at first sight look like birds. They are what are popularly known as witches' blooms or brooms. In the opinion of a writer in the Museum. Gazette, they are usually the results of some bygone disturb- ancs in the economy of a shoot-producing bud, which caused the bud to break up into several instead of continuing its onward growth as a single branch. The irritating in- fluence which produced this result was pro- bably applied at a very early period of the bud formation, while its development was but little advanced. Whether due to insect or fungal irritation these brooms are not in- fective to the rest of the tree, and do not cause decay, or injure the tree in any way beyond uselessly consuming a certain amount of sap. It is possible that some of the best " brooms " are caused by the attacks of 'cites which petsist in their occupancy and attack the spnng buds every succeeding year.

THE GROWTH OF RADIUM.

An important letter has appeared in Nature from Professor Rutherford as to ex- periments on the growth of radium in pre- parations of actinium. The rate of growth, he says, was found, to be uniform over a period of 120 days, and to agree closely with that observed in the solid preparation of ac- tinium which had been set aside for a period of two and a-half years. Another sample of actinium was then taken and successively precipitated with ammonium sulphide in order to remove the radium from the solu- tion. In this way a solution of actinium was obtained initially althost entirely free from radium.

By examination of the a-ray. activity it was found that the actinium after this chemical treatment contained an excess of radio-ac- tinium. This was .shewn by the rise of the activity to twice its initial value in about twenty. days, and then a gradual decay to a steady value. Special care was taken to measure accurately the RATE OF GROWTH dr RADIUM

at Elort intervals, to see whether it de- pended upon the variation of the activity. INTI such connection was observed, for the radium was • produced at a constant rate over the whole period of examination. In conclu- sion, Professor • '.Rutherford sums up by re- marking that while he intends to continue his experiments, it may now be safely con- cluded that in the ordinary commercial pre- parations of actinium there exists a new sub- stance which is slowly transformed into radium. This immediate parent of radium is chemically quite distinct from actinium and radium and their known products, and is capable of complete separation from them. It. is, however, not yet possible to decide de- finitely whether this parent substance is a final produot of the transformation of ac- tinium or net.. It is not improbable that it may prove to be the long-looked-for inter- mediate product of slow stransformation be- t.ween uranium X and mdicin, but with no elireet radio active connectibn with actinium.

THE MOON AND THE PACIFIC.

In Harper's 2:l4.agaeihe for June there ap- peared a striking article from the pen af Professor W. H. Pickering, tht well-known Aracrienn astronomer. Sir G. H. Darwin discovered, twenty-eight years ago, that. this moon was in all probability origioally a por- tion of the earth, which . was detachNl from the surface of our planet owing to its rapid rotation millions of years ago. This rotation —which was performed in a period of be- tween three and tiro hours was so rapid that the earth split in two, and one-half was driven kora its surface to form the moon. Professor Pickering gocs a step further, and attempts to fix the Fortion of otzr planet from which the moon Was detached. He fixes on the bed of the Pacific Ocean as the place from which the moon was lined into space.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
How IT HAPPENED

Prolessor Pickering describes the memea- tops event in these words: "As the part of the earth's crust near the present islands began to riSe, in obediexee to the centrifugal force developed ~by the earth's rotation, the crust on the opposite side cracked and split in two. Before the crack could widen more than 2,000 miles, the pull became so intense that a huge, roughly - circular piece, forming three-qUarters of the earth's whole crust, was taken out of the. middle and carried eway to form the moon." He finds the ave- rage ,density of the vmoon to be nearly the same as that of the earth's surface, and this is further evidence that the ma:terials now forming the moon once filled the bed of the Pacific. Professor Pickering .points out that if the moon had never been formed at all, or if i; had carried away. the whole of the ter- restrial crust, our earth would then have been completely enveloped in its oceans. "Our race could then hardly have advanced beond the 'intelligence of the present deep. IC& fish."

THE LATE MR. L. W. REYNOLDS.

Mrs. Reynolds wrote expressing her sincere thanks for the kind sympathy shown to her by the Committee in her bereavement.

ASSOCIATION OF EDUCATION COMMITTEES.

An intaresting report was presented by the repre- sentatives to the annual meeting of the Association of Education Committees recently held in London. Among the matters dealt with was the following : The Conference resolved to make strong representations to che Board of Edueation pointing out the loss of Income to Local Authorities owing to diminished school attendance arising from infections and other diseases. This is a matter which intimately concerns us. Owing to the closer relationship between the Health Department and the Education Committee, more efficiency is obtained and many children are rightly excluded from school on account of sickness or con- tact with infectious or other diseases but under the present conditions the more vigilant the Local Authority is in endeavouring to prevent the spread of disease the more severely it has to suffer by loss of grant.and it is hoped that some alteration will soon be made whereby duly authenticated cases may be counted tor the average attendance. Commenting upon the report, the CHAIRMAN expressed the hope that they would be successful in their endeavours to force the Board of Education to move the wheels a little more quickly than they had been doing in the past. A REQUEST TO THE BOROUGH MEMBER.

The Finance Committee resolved to recommend that the local Member of Parliament (Hr. A. H. Crosfield) be requested to support a motion which is shortly to be proposed in Parliament with a view to inducing the Government to sanction a longer period than 30 years for the repayment of loans for the erection of school buildings.

Mr. JOLLEY moved the adoption of the minutes, Councillor WILKINSON seconded, and the minutes were passed. THE BIBLE IN COUNCIL SCHOOLS. SYLLABUS APPROVED.

The School Management Committee submitted for approve!" general regulations respecting Bible instruc- tion and religious observances in Council schools." Amongst the various regulations were the following: —That the Bible shall be read, and explanations and instructions given therefrom in the principles of Christian religion and of morality as are suited to the capacities of the children. No attempt shall be made either to detach children from, or to attach them twiny particular denomination. The first half-hour of every morning, and the last five or ten minutes of every afternoon's session of school shall be devoted to Buell observances and instruction. In the event of any parent or guardian objecting to his or her child being present during the time of religious instruction, the child must be withdrawn and receive instruction in secular subjects in a separate room. Canon Stevenson not having arrived at this stage of the proceedings, the confirmation of the minutes was moved by Mr. UPSON and seconded by Councillor PAMBEIBTON.

Councillor BALL moved that the minute approving the religious syllabus be not confirmed. He said he hoped he would be able to rely with all modesty and humility of spirit on his record of service in religious work to absolve him from any charge of irreligion or disregard of religious interests in the matter. It had been tried for many years to evolve a scheme of religious instruction that would not give offence to any particular partisan and hitherto all attempts had failed. With all due respect to the present syllabus, which he thought was an admirable one of its sort, he feared there would he openings left for sectarian dissatisfaction and they would have the same old trouble recurring in the schools that they had had for so long past. It might easily happen, because the teacher was not asked to simply read the Bible, give out a hymn, or lead in prayer,but he was asked to explain the Bible selections. When they entered into explanations they immediately entered on a system of denominational and sectarian interpretation of the Bible and the characters therein. Ittwould probably happen that if by any chance a number of Church teachers were in a Council school they would have a Nonconformist sectarian objecting and thinking that a bias of the wrong sort was being given to the teaching. On the other hand if the majority of teachers were Nonconformists, members of the Church of England would probably be raising a similar objection. Teaching of a similar nature and also of a more denominational character had been given in the schools for a long time and yet they found the results as regarded the lives of the people as a whole were not to be compared with the amount of time and energy expended on the teaching. Their ministers deplored to-day the lack of religion among the mass of the people, their indiffer- ence to Church worship and religious observance generally, and if the result of past teaching was so paltry he did not see why they should run the risk of creating sectarian strife for such a small result. It also seemed to him that they were indirectly putting a certain amount of religious test on the teachers if they were to be asked to teach any kind of religion, no matter how simple. There would probably remain a number of teachers upon whom religion had no claim whatever from intellectual and other reasons. He did not think teachers as a body were any more capable of teaching the truths of religion than an outsider generally was. He objected to any paid religious service that was not supplemented by a natural spiritual call because he believed that the teaching, to be of any force in the lives of the children, must be helped forward by a natural spiritual i impulse n the teachers. The mere teaching of religion as an ordinary part of the school curriculuua would not, he feared, have anything like the religious and good effect they wished it to have. Mr. PLINSTON seconded the amendment. Mr. 11. ROBERTS said that so far as the spirit in which Mr. Ball had moved the amendment was concerned, he had not the slightest fault to find with it. In matter and manner he had put the case excellently, and he (Mr. Roberts) would be largely disposed to agree with him in principle, but he did not agree with the deductions Mr. Ball had made from certain facts. He (Mr. Roberts) I did not agree that because clergymen and ministers were largely bemoaning the fact that attendance at ' church and chapel was not what they would like it to be and what, judging by the • percentages it used to be years ago, that therefore it was necessarily any direct evidence of the spread of irreligion amongst the people. To his mind the country was far more more Christian to-day than over it had been in its histor).. as shown perhaps by less theoretical but by far more numerous instances of practical Christianity. He attributed that very largely to the inculcation of real religion among the children, from whatever source. He hoped the time would come when they would agree that it was not the part of the duty of the State, either through schools or any other instrumentality, to teach religion, but that time was not yet. A Nonconformist, he was in entire sympathy with a large proportion of the arguments used by Councillor Ball, hut he would prefer the principles of religion being taught,as they would be, by secularly-minded men to a certain extent rather tl.4an not taught at all. He had the highest regard for the teachers of the schools en masse at men and women of high character, and he did not think they would by their method of treating, the matter secularize the religious teaching. He did not object to the teaching of denominational religion in schools privately provided and at the expense of the denominations, but he did object to the State lending any aid to the teaching of religion of a dogmatic or sectarian character, and in their Council sohools they could avoid that danger if they cared to do so. If they could incalculate in the children a higher sense of duty I to themselves and to the State and the district in which they lived they would be better citizens, better fathers i and mothers:

The Rev. CANON STEVIIVSON said that as the chairman of the School Management Committee, he was largely responsible for the drawing up of the syllabus. He associated himself with what had been said by Mr. Roberts as to the spirit in which Councillor Ball had spoken. From his point of view Councillor Ball had put his case as nicely and as clearly as it could be put, and he vgreed to a large extent with what he had said, as Nvoll as with nearly all that had been said by the previous speaker. He had no brief for the kind of religious teaching given In the Council school. He wished to see every child taught what his parents wished him or her to be taught. They had a certain law and they had to work loyally to it. The law permitted a certain amount of religious teaching to be given in the Council schools ard the question was" Shall we avail ourselves of it, shall we make that religious teaching as good as it can be made or cut the whole thing out

and have none at all ?" When he put that question to himself he could only find one answer. He looked at many of the homes of the children and he knew, as they all knew,that if they were to trust to the amount of religious teaching they would get those homes it would be lamentably little if any at all ; and he for one because he could not get all he should like to get, was not going to shut the door to such religious teaching as might be given. If the committee passed the scheme before them he should endeavour to see that the religious teaching was given in the best possible way. With reference to the remark which had been made about the teachers, he thought it would be airaitted that he (Canon Steven- son) had had considerable experience of them. They valued the ,privilege of being able to give such lessons as might inculcate in the children a higher sense of duty and some elementary knowledge of the great principles of the Christian religion, as well as to instil into them some love for the Lord Jesus Christ. The influence of religious teaching throughout the land had been of inoalculable value, and it would not be wise to deprive themselves of it. It had been said that not so many , people went to church or chapel now. That was a coin- plex question. There were many causes to be con- sidered, but if what had been said was true about the attendance, was it not all the more necessary to try to bring religious influences to bear upon the young whilst they could be moulded? (Hear, hear.) Mr. PARKBR said the principal danger to the State was that children should grow up to be immoral or without any morals, and it seemed, therefore, to be necessary for the State to safeguard itself in regard to the morality of the children. And, osier as they knew , there was nothing from which morality could derive its ' strength and purpose better than a belief in the Great Father and a knowledge of the revelation of Himself in His Son. Therefore it seemed to him that it was the duty of the State to teach children morality. Believing as he did, and the majority they represented believing it, that ethical teaching had not force unless backed up by a belief in the elements of their common religion, it seemed to him to be the duty of the State —and they represented a section of it—to teach religion i and in the simple form incorporated in the syllabus , before thetn. He did not think Councillor Ball need ' fear sectarian differences. There would always be a few people to disagree with things, but for the sake of the small minority who would disagree with minute points would it be right to eliminate the most impor- tant points? There were people who would object to anything. (Laughter.) He hoped the committee would approve the syllabus, beoanse he believed it repre- sented what the great majority of the people in the town, whether closely associated with religious teach- ing or not, desired their children should be taught. Mr. PLISSTON was afraid the Committee themselves considered there might be something farcical about the religious teaching or they would not have appended the note about it being given in a reverent spirit. He did not believe in a religion driven home at the point of the cane. (Laughter.) Gentlemen might smile, but , too often it had been done, and he believed it was being I done to-day. Mr. LTrsoN said that with much Couneillor Ball had , said he most cordially agreed, and he had only risen to say one thing, and that was that theoretically while it looked probable that a sectarian colour would be given by teachers in the interpretation of the Word of God his teaching experience had proved just the contrary. They had had over 34 years of the Board schools and he did not know that a charge bad ever been brought against the teachers of giving teaching of a sectarian colour. If Councillor Ball would believe him, it was a I marvel to him that there was so little sectarian dispute, ' so little trouble over religious matters in the schools. (Hoar, hear.) He attributed that entirely to the tact I aid wisdom of the teachers entrusted with the giving of the religious instruction. He Wrii referring particu- larly to the Voluntary schools. Many thousands of Nonconformist parents sent their children, sometimes of necessity, to church schools, and they had no objec- tion at all to the teaching given because of the wisdom , of the man or woman who imparted the instruction, and if the rev, gentlemen would forgive him he would say that if they would all keep out of the way and leave it to the parents and teachers there would be no religious difficulty whatever. (Laughter.) Religious instruction was being given in a thoroughly serious and reverent spirit, and he thought the note at the end of the instruc- tion 3. might be withdrawn..

Alderrican BENTSBTT said he could not see any serious objection to the admirable syllabus which had been , prepared. Personally, he found it somewhat difficult to believe in the existence of a secular teacher. He did not think it was possible for anyone to be absolutely secular unless he was an absolute machine. There was so much in this world that was mysterious and awe- inspiring that he could not imagine any teacher being wholly secular, and who could understand the beautiful i Hnglish of the Bible without treating the book with a ; certain amount of respect. It would bean incalculable ; loss if they were to banish this kind of teaching from the schools. They devoted enormous time and atten- tion and spent enormous sums of money in teaching things which were not so important. He thought one reason why people were ceasing to go to church was that they thoupt they could understand Christ's teach- info better without the ecclesiastical interpretations I super-imposed upon it. Those who objected to the I teaching of the Bible were really in such a small I minority that they were almost a negligible quantity. ' He did not think it was fair because of a small minority that the vast majority should be overlooked. Mr. JOLLBY said if the amendment were carried it I meant that the Bible was to be excluded Isom the , Council schools. Any policy which would lead to the exclusion of the Bible from the elementary schools of the country would be, in his opinion, reactionary and gravely injurious. Alderman TINNION supported the minutes. He said they might congratulate themselves in Warrington that. sectarian differences had been conspicuous by their absence. He thought the syllabus was a very happy compromise, and they could try it for twelve months. I It was the first Council school in which religions instruction was to be given under the direction of the ; Hducation Committee, and they could make this an , experiment. The conditions were in his opinion fair ; all round.

The Rev. JOHN YOWOIII said he had been asked by several people who drew the syllabus up, and they looked at him as if they thought he had. (Laughter.) He was in absolute ignorance on the question, but it had been dispelled by the candid acknowledgment made by Canon Stevenson that he had had the honour of drawing up the syllabus. Canon STIvENSON said he would not take an honour that did not belong to him. He said he was largely responsible for the syllabus as chairman of the sub- committee. It really was composed from syllabuses submitted from other places. He did not want credit for what he had not done.

The Rev. JOHN YONGX said he thought it was an admirable syllabus, and he hoped it would find its way into the curriculum of Wycliffe School—but that was a pious hope. He asked whether—seeing that the ques- tion of education was in Parliament and that they were promised a measure next session that was to deal in the most complete manner with the whole matter—it would not be wise to let that matter be hung up until they knew what the national scheme was likely to he. He did not know that they would lose naneh by that, and they might avoid the necessity of striking out the resolution which might be carried that night. If he (Mr. Yonge) were driven to vote upon the question whether the introduction of this syllabus was a right and proper thing to do in face of strong Nonconformist objection, he would vote with his Nonconformist friends. He would do so with exceeding great regret, because he could not conceive that it would work mischievously for the children concerned. One of their strong Nonconformist principles was that the State ought not to have anything to do with the teaching of religion as a State, and he would have to vote on that prinniple. He had hoped the syllabus might have passed through, and that they might have seen how it would work. He had been informed that the working of this kind of thing had been eminently successful in various parts of the country. The Rev. JAMAS DAVIIN pointed out that if the parents of any child did not wista that religious instruc- tion should be given, that child could be withdraws from the lesson and given some secelar subject. There were thousands who desired religions instruction to be given. and if the amendment were passed they could not have it. if religious instruction was withdrawn they were putting a ban upon the Bible, and it would bring discredit on the town. He suggested that the amend- ment should be withdrawn.

Councillor PEMBERTON supported the minutes on the same grounds as Alderman Bennett. It would be deplorable, he said, if sorsa religious teaching was not given. They had too little charity in this world, and too little of the practice of Christianity. Councillor TOOLE said in the Catholic sch3ols it had always been religion first and last, and he hoped it wonld always be so in all their schools, no matter the denomination. If he had to send his own children to the Council school, he would be in favour of this syllabus rather than that no religion should be taught— (hear, hear)—although perhaps there were some things he might not agree with. He hoped religion would Rot be driven out of the schools.

The CrrAirtmAN- vs-ilid-pernally he would he sorry if there was any withdrawal of religion from the sch3ols. Education was not complete witnout a good reli4ioue training.

For the ainendmont only three voted (lr. Ball. Mr. Plinston, and the Rev. John Yonge), rd it was 16st by an overwhelming majority. LOCAL EXAMINATTON RESULTS. _ DELAY IN PUBLTCATION.

Mr. PLINSTON called attention to the minute:—" The result of the losel examination recently held for the admission of pupil teachers was submitted, and it was resolved that the same be forwarded to the Board ot Education for their approval." He would like some information, he said, as to how it was possible for a number of reports to he circulated about Warrington with regard to the results. Some children could be told as to whether they had passed or failed. A num- ber of people had approached him as to whether he know anything about the results. He told them he knew nothirg. Several members had also been asked the same question and had not bean ablo to answer it. He was surprised to find a resolution so termed in face of the information that had been spread round the town about the results. He thought that it' this had been done in a surreptitious manner by a member who had got knowledge it was most deplorable and he hoped it would be stopped. Mr. UPSON said he had told several persons what

the result of the examination had been. There was no secrecy about tho matter. The result of the examina- tion was presented to the committee, and was known to the members. The fact that it had to go to the Board of Education mattered nothing. Parents wrote to him and came to see him, anxiously imploring to know whether their children had passed or failed. Why should he play the mystery man because the list had been sent to the Board of Education for an office boy to look at ? (Laughter). After further discussion, Canon STIWENSON said they were not responsible for the long delay, nor had they any means to suppose there would be such delay. He thought he could undertake to say that they would in such circumstances be glad to make the list public, with a reservation that it was subject to the approval of the Board of Education.

PAYMENT OF SCHOOL-KEEPERS' WAGES. MR. A. J. JOLLEY'S INSINUATIO N.

Mr. T. P. BQADSHINT moved :—" That it be an instruction to the Finance Sub-committee to arrange for the wages of school-keepers to be paid on Friday afternoon instead of Monday as at present." He under- stood that in August last 15 out of 115 male school- keepers sent in a petition with a view to getting their wages paid on Saturday instead of Monday, but the Finance Sub-committee could not conveniently make the alteration. He (Mr. Bradshaw) now found that if a change was to be made Friday was a better day than Saturday for the wages to be paid. If the wages were paid on Friday the money would be better utilized for household purposes than it would on a Monday, because Saturday was market day and things were cheaper. He understood that the school-keepers dated the receipts for wages Saturday even though they were paid on a Monday. Councillor PI3MBERTON seconded. Mr. JOLLIET said if the resolution was passed it meant that the school-keepers would have to forego their wages for a week. If the matter was brought torward in another form and in the proper spirit it would have attention. In its present form it looked like a vulgar dodge to got a cheap bit of popularity at the ratepayers' expense.___ _

.oouncillor PEMBRREON (warmly) : It is not a vulgar dodge,and I strongly resent it. Those are very improper remarks.

r. PLINSTON also took exception to Mr. Jolley's attempts to put down individual members of the com- mittee. Mr. Jolley had on many occasions attempted to stigmatize the action of members of that authority as being improper. He must be an expert at dodgery if he supposed dodgery was at the root of the resolution. Whether they differed from Mr. Jolley or not he (Mr. Plinston) believed the members came there to do their duty. gr. Jolley attempted to put down prople's throats wrong motives and be (Mr. Plinston) resented it, and hoped that when such attacks were made again the Chairman would call Mr.Jolley to order. Counoillor STEHL said he was sorry Mr. Jolley had broached the matter with such warmth. Personally,he saw no difficulty for the sub-committee to apportion a week's wages, and say the school-keepers should be paid up to Friday for the first week, and then weekly from Friday to Friday afterwards. Councillor NIELD expressed a similiar opinion as to the adjustment of the wages. Mr. BRADSII.A.W said there was no need to reply after the remarks of the Chairman of the " intelligence department."

The motion was then carried without opposition.

The Examiner, Saturday, June 22, 1907.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]'

"At least you are not ping away to-multrow ?" "No, not to-morrow." "Then we shall meet again," he said, decisively. _ _

Clare smiled. She called her dogs, and with a 'ittle friendly nod departed. The young map remained standing by the stile for some minutes after her departure. Though he was an artist he pulled out no sketch-book, nor did he open a paint-box, but he just drank in thirstily all the beauty of te eminently English scene—the soft, dewy atmosphere; the sweet earth smell; the pale blue sky, where little white clouds raced airily along. The song of the lark overhead filled him with divine pleasure. He opened his mouth and inhaled great breaths, of the pure air. Then at last he turned away. "Ah, it is good," he murmured—"very good!" as he strode quickTy down the hill. - Clare met her new friend several times after that.. Occasionally they walked together, always they talked, unconventionally, pleasantly, at random. Such random talks are worth centuries of average drawing-room conversation—yet how rare they are! The artist was an episode in her life, and must always remain so. He told her he was going away soon on business, that he had only come to the village to look round, to prospect, as he called it, to make sketches. He would return but not just yet. Then Clare told him her story; how the odious new American landlord intended to turn them out of house and home, and how bitterly her mother felt it. Ho listened with the deepest interest, scarcely moving a muscle, almost impassive, but keeping his eyes fixed on her bright, ever-changing face. "Do you really think he knows," he asked once, "how much misery he is causing ? "

"Of course. Rich men think of nothing but themselves and their own pleasures. No doubt he wants our house for his gardener or his steward; or perhaps he will build new cast-iron stables, or a set of hideous flats over the dear old garden." "I should hardly think that," said the artist, quietly. "After all, even if he is net a man of taste himself (and I grant you these self-made men rarely are), yet he will probably have some competent person to advise him." "Ah, if he only had you ! " sighed Clare. "But that is impossible." "Quite impossible. How could I advise a vulgar millionaire? I suppose he is vulgar ? " "Of course. Why should he want to come here, to this quiet, out-of-the-way place? He could have as much of the things that suited him —of London dust and noise and motors—as he wished, elsewhere." "Suppose he was tired of dust and noise and motors—suppose it was just rest he wanted ? " "Oh, no, that kind of man hates rest. They say business men die when they go out of harness." Clare thought of her father, and a half- suppressed sob rose in her throat. "Perhaps, then, he will die and you will stay on." lie gave her a quick, pathetic look. "That would be the best way out of the dilemma."

The artist went. Clare, in spite of herself, missed him terribly. She had grown to want those long irresponsible talks, brightened by the ;artist's rare smiles and his marvellous appreciation. June passed in glorious majesty. Nights of clear starlight succeeded balhly sunshiny days, when it became a pleasure just to exist, to sit and bask like a cat in the still warm garden among the roses and jasmine. Had it not been for the aching care at the back of her thoughts Clare would have been happy. As it was, when July came, and the weather grew more sultry, she abandoned the gay morning expeditions. The very dogs lost their spirits. They mooned about languidly,and lay all day in the shade with rosy tongues lolling out of their mouths and their sides heaving uncomfortably. Mrs. Findon pored over lists of furniture and registers of houses to let. Where to live became the burning question of the hour. Clare hated the idea of a suburban villa; she hated still more the idea of a small house in some mean street where the milk- cans hang on the area railings, and the half- starved cats slink furtively round the corner. Mrs. Findon pined for town. It would be more cheerful she thought in the winter, Clare could see a little company, and people would drop in to tea. Clare knew what kind of people drop in to tea with a widow in straitened circumstances living in a row of houses, each house with its short white muslin blinds, its pot of mignonette or red geraniums on the window-sill, and the washing hanging out in the back garden. She shuddered. The Laurels, with its humble rent, was at least old and homely, and savoured of none of-the sordid details of real poverty. But now—in the new London life there would be no room for dogs—even they must go. At that thought Clare felt a deadly sickness at heart. They had been her dumb, patieut, loving friends; they had romped with her, rejoiced with her; hung lovingly on her kind words, and licked her hands. No! there was no real friend like a dog. Even the artist had forsaken her. He had promised to return, he had promised to write, and he had done neither—and she did not even know his name! It was August, and the time of their quitting close at hand. Already the house was being dig,- mantled. Packing-cases littered the floor; book- shelves gaped in dreary emptiness; pictures were torn ruthlessly from the faded walls; a miserable noise of hammering and sense of discomfort reigned in the serene quiet of the peaceful old house. The neighbours shewed their kindness; the doctor's wife brought some powders her husband recommended to purify the doubtful London drinking water; the clergyman advised as to the storing and removal of furniture; the little old-maid district visitor, Miss Letitia Crisp, offered her services in the timid manner of the hopeless spinster; and now at last the garrulous banker appeared with a real piece of news. "Mr. Cyrus P. Andrews, the new landlord, has arrived," he said. "There's a chance for you Mrs. Findon, to see him before you go." "What good will that do me ? " the widow asked. She had grown calmer and more querulous with all the worry of packing. "He will bespatter us with the mud of his fine carriage, and sicken us with the sight of his money." "You might perhaps obtain an extension of your lease," suggested the good-natured banker. "Who knows, if Miss Clare will ask him, he might consent ! " "Do you think I would let my daughter ask a favour of such a man, a man who has probably robbed the orphan and the, widow all his life, and has fattened on their prayers and their misery? Never 1" "It is not so bad as that, I am sure."

"Besides, he knows we want to stay. I wrote to his solicitor and prayed for an extension of lease, and he never even answered. He is doing it on purpose to spite us. We are only poor helpless women." Oh! mother dear," put in Clare, "I don't believe that. He probably knows nothing about it, and leaves it all to the solicitor." Mrs. Findon leant back obstinately in her chair, and the discussion ended, but Clare pondered over it in the solitude of her own room. The banker was right. Why should she not plead with this new obdurate American landlord? No harm could come and perhaps some good might ensue. Her pride was pretty well mated now, and if he did refuse her, even rudely, why hard words break no bones. So next day she dressed herself nicely, but plainly, and without a word to her mother, leaving the dogs behind, she started. to walk to the Manor House. A short drive led up to the beautiful old Queen Anne building, enclosed in its grassy, well-timbered park, and with the wide flight of steps branching out as in some old French mansion" on each side of the entrance door. Clare rang the bell nervously. It was answered by a quiet, respectable old man- servant, who replied that Mr. Andrews was at 'home, but he did not know if his master would receive visitors. Clare tendered him her card. The servant led her upstairs and bade her wait in a large' painted hall, with black-and-white marble floor. Clare looked round curiously. -So this was the millionaire's home, stately and in perfect taste, the primitive furniture fine and solid, no frippery or gilding marring the perfect harmony of the high, white chimney-piece reaching to the ceiling, or the splendid painted ceiling itself. The man who lived here could be no common, self-made man; the very curtains of a deep rich sombre red shewed unerring taste and propriety. Clare began to 'doubt. Perhaps, after all, he might be amenable to reason—he might understand what it cost her to leave her quaint and simple home.

i The servant re-entering said, respectfully: "Mr. Andrews will see you, miss, in his study. Please to steo this way_ Clare followed down a couple of winding passages, up a few stairs, through a red-baize door into a panelled room, where at a large writing-table covered with papera Bat a man, who rose at her entrance.

Wasting and Palpitation. FELL FROM A SCAFFOLD.

..

Mr. Thos. Stephens Hamer, of 24. Prince-street, Higher Bridge-street, Bolton, writes :—" For the last four ) ears I suffered agonies from shattered nerves and general bodily wasting and weakness. My dread- ful state was owing to a fall off a scaffold. which :educed me to a mere wreck. I could not eat, and this only tended to make me lose flesh more.l suffered dread- fully from sick headache, got weaker and weaker, and when I walked I had bad heart palpitation. "I was in this seemingly hopeless condition when I began to try Dr. Cassell's Tablets, and was astonished to find what a trial bottle only did for me. The sickness left me, and I could eat better and my food did me more good. I did not feel so tired and Tak, and felt altogether better. I continued the 2ablets, and n3w I can thankfully say I am quite red. I can walk and work with pleasure and comfort, and I nd strength and have put on r3lb. in weight. I consider Dr. Cassell's-Tablets are a wonderful remedy."

(Prescot), Dodd (Liverpool), Marsh (Lowton), Allen (Platt Bridge), Howarth (Bolton), Bottomley (Batley), Kendrick (St. Helens Junction), and the other officers, Executive Committee. - THE INCORPORATION QUESTION.

The question of incorporation was introduced by Mr. H. Brimelow (Bolton), who moved —"That this meet- ing of representatives of Independent Methodist churches, hereby records its deep regret that, notwith- standing the protests of many churches in the Union, and in disregard of all appeals for further consideration and discussion, the incorporation under the Companies Acts' 1862-1900 of a limited company entitied The Independent Methodist Association (Incorporated) ' has been effected for the purposes named in the Memorandum of Association. We affirm our belief that this placing of our connexional affairs under Acts of Parliament passed for the regulation of limited liability companies. is :—(1) In direct conflict with the funda- mental principles and aims of our denomination, (2) An encroachment upon the control of the annual meet- ing over the property and funds of the Connexion; (3) An infringement of loug-established rights and privileges of circuits, churches and members; and (5) opposed to Scriptural ideal. For these and other reasons, which constitute differences on fundamental principles, we find ourselves unable to countenance the Association, and we hereby further affirm our determination to co-operate, by such means as may from time to time be deemed necessary, in maintaining inviolate and promot- ing the principles, aims and polity of the Independent Methodist denomination." (Applause.) In the course of a long speech he ably reviewed the history of the question. They were met, he said, for the first time as a constituted body and that occasion would probably be historic. This conflict had been none of their choosing and they opposed incorporation purely from conscientious grounds. The step had been taken in spite of their serious protests and their pleas for further discussion. That the matter bad not been discussed with any adequacy by the churches they were receiving fresh evidence every day. He contended that the resolutions passed at the Sunderland Con- ference of 1903 which it was said affirmed the principle, were not definite and conclusive and not morally binding upon the churches. The financial position of the Connexion did not call for any such drastic methods. Even if something had to be done in connection with the management of the churches incorporation was not the best thing from a business point of view. The Connexional funds were not as safe to-day under an association as they would be in the hands of two personal trusters who could be trusted. This matter had been rushed at more after the manner of a bull in a china shop than anything else. What objection could there be to the appointment of five trustees with the proviso that if they were reduced to three the number should be immediately made up again to Ire? That could be accompanied with special regulations and a declaration of trust to be made by the trustees. That was a simple procedure and would secure to them their property as safely as anything in the world could secure it; and then there would have been no division. To those present this was a matter of conviction and principle; minorities and majorities did not enter into it at all. They believed they were standing for a God-given principle. The 1 registration of the company was tantamount to placing I their denomination, aims, and principles of work under Acts of Parliament formed for joint-stock company work and nothing else. Their privileges were curtailed their rights disturbed and their freedom largely robbed. The constitution was also opposed to the scriptural ideal of a church and was an encroachment on liberty. The essence of law was coercion, the essence of Christianity was love, and force and love were contrasts. Every Christian organization should manage its own affairs,and be independent of everyone but the Supreme Head the Lord Jesus Christ: (Applause). To put their religious organizations under laws relat- ing to trading companies was to violate the scriptural idea of the working of a church. From 1832 to 1888 no fewer than 229 Acts of Parlia- ment had to be passed to enable the Church of England to carry on its work, and under the arrangement in their own denomination, if they wanted to do anything not provided for in the articles they would have to go to law to obtain permission. They did not want to see division in the church, but to do other than they had done would be disloyal to the principles in which they had been born and reared. (Applause.) Their one motive was loyalty to the connexion and he trusted they would not take an aggressive attitude, but, as in the past, act in self defence. (Appause.) Mr. W. WRIGIIT, Warrington, seconded, and said the speech they had just heard would not only be of service to them there that day, but it might have its influence with their body in the future. He was sure the meeting felt its indebtedness to Mr. Brimelow for the very wide, impressive, and conclusive way in which he had dealt with the question. (Hear, hear.) Everything should be done in the best spirit, and with a good feel- ing to all these who differed from them so that the blessing of God might-rest upon their efforts. He some- times wondered whether those who stood at the bead of affairs really understood Independent Methodism as they ought to do. The important point to bear in mind was that if they allowed their polity to be interfered with, there was a danger of doctrines being tampered with too. It should be their deepest concern to pre- serve both intact. A discussion followed, the speakers including Messrs WARD (Liverpool), GERRARD (Lowton Common), ISTF,RLING (LiverpOOI).WARING (Haydock), HOUGHTON (Wigan), DAVENPORT (Tyldesley), HOWARTH (Bolton), and the PRESIDENT. The resolution was put to the meeting and carried unanimously, after which the oonference was adjourned for luncheon.

AFTERNOON PROCEEDINGS.

At the opening of the proceedings in the afternoon Mr. BRIMELOW moved "That this meeting confirms the resolutions passed at previous conferences of represen- tatives of churches opposed to the registration of the Independent Methodist Association (Incorporated). We affirm out belief that compliance by the Connexional Trustees with the request made to them by letter dated March 30th, 1907, to transfer to the association the investments held by them on behalf of the Independent Methodist Connexion (to whose funds the churches represented at this meeting and their members have liberally contributed) would be a departure from the constitution and conditions under which those funds were contributed. We further affirm our conviction that no portion of stich funds can be diverted to the use of any body or association not adhering to and maintaining intact the fundamental principles and objects for the 'furtherance of which those funds have been accumulated. This meeting, therefore' calls upon the Connexional Trustees to retain the investments held by them on behalf of the Connexion until the legal position has been been determined, or the matter otserwise settled, and hereby authorizes the trustees to take such steps as they may be advised are necessary to give effect to this resolution." Mr. S. DALE (Warrington) seconded. He said they would not have contributed to the funds had they known that there was going to be a departure from the old free system of things under which the free gospel was to be propagated. The trustees who held those funds were in rather a peculiar position. A majority did not make si thing right or the position of the trustees safe. He supposed they would have to make application to a certain court to know how they stood, and that the cost of that application would come out of the funds to which those present had contributed.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
IN AMERICAN LANDLORD.

Mrs. Findon sat by the window in an attitude of deepest dejection. In vain the Gloire de Dijon roses filled the air with fragrance and nodded their heavy heads sleepily towards her; in vain the canary-bird trilled and sang his best in the splendour of his gilded cage, in eager --NAlation of the happy, free wild birds outside; fain summer spoke with all its keenest and most passionate accents of love and hope and happiness. Mrs. Findon sat as one turned to, stone. She hardly moved her head when the door opened and a tall, brown-haired girl entered with a graceful swing of petticoats. "Well, mother," she said, unceremoniously tossing her hat into the nearest chair, "has anything happened since I went out ? " "Everything has happened!" answered her mother, in despairing tones. "The notice to quit from the new landlord has come--" "Oh, mother ! " The girl stood motionless, her breath corning quickly. "Yes, it's come. I knew it would when I heard the estate had changed hands. Disagree- able things always do happen to me, and this is the worst—to be turned out of this little nest, where I hoped to die in peace, for to die is all an old woman like me can do ! " The girl did not speak. "Why don't you answer ? " her mother rambled on. "-You don't care, though my heart is breaking—young things are so selfish! I daresay you'll like the change." Clare Findon looked at her mother. Her face was calm and pale, but she pressed her hands . together nervously. "It can't—it can't be true!" she said. "It is true. Here, read." Clare took the letter. It was clear as day—a notice to quit at the end of three months. "'We have three months still," Clare said, hopefully, folding up the paper. "What is three months ? " "So much can happen in three months." I "I might die," said her mother, querulously. ' "Dr. Bright told me my heart was weak." "Oh! mother, you know I don't mean that." "Then there's all the furniture to move— that's awful! And where can we go? Oh, it will be my death—l know it will !" "The landlord may change his mind," suggested Clare, hopefully. "Never! Those Americans are so determined. Your father once owed an American some money. He had to pay it without delay, principal and interest. He was the worst creditor your poor father ever had—and goodness knows he had plenty!" "I have heard that Americans are good men of business," said Clare, thoughtfully. "But why should he care to have this house, I wonder? It's only a poor little place, we are good tenants, and we pay our rent." "How should I know I tate the word business. Business killed your father, and business will kill me." "Don't, mother; there is always room for hope," "And time to die, added Mrs. Findon, bitterly. "You're strong and hard, like your father. You don't understand." It was true that Clare was strong, else bow could she have borne the burden of her mother's misfortunes? Mr. Findon, a shrewd and clever man, had speculated a little too boldly, lost heavily, then, in a moment of despair, shot himself, leaving wife and child almost penniless. It was then that Clare, a mere girl of eighteen, showed herself strong and capable. She gathered together the threads of his deplorably complicated affairs, and,with the help of a solicitor, paid all his debts, leaving herself only a pittance. She and 'her mother drifted from a condition of affluence into the little home at the Laurels, where they had lived since then. The rambling old house, once a cottage' built on to and enlarged by each succeeding tenant, held its own special charm. The pannelled rooms, the queer little irregular steps, the low ceilings, the roses grown oriel windows, the large old shady garden, sloping down to the pond where the water- lilies grew, and bordered by a wall where flourished sweet-smelling gully-flowers, satisfied Clare's artistic instincts. She had grown very fond of its quiet and dignified repose, so soothing to her harassed nerves after the terrible experiences of bankruptcy and suicide. Her nerves were rested now. She was in the full flush of womanhood, looking out on life with eager and appreciative eyes. Mrs. Findon had tasted- peace after the tragedy of her husband's death, and to her a new upheaval meant a fresh tragedy; and for her mother's sake Clare dreaded a change. The two poor women sat together talking till late into the night, Clare still trying to hope against hope, Mrs. Findon characteristically abandoned to despair. Next day Clare rose early, and calling her dogs went out into the village. She was fond of long rambles in the fresh morning air, when she met only a few matutinal rustics, and the dogs could enjoy a free and boisterous run. The girl restrained herself nobly to be gentle with her mother's peevishness. But sometimes youth asserted itself, and the joy of life bubbled up in her veins. She wanted to run and jump, climb over stiles, and throw sticks for the dogs into the trout stream. The dogs thoroughly entered into their mistress's spirit of fun, and sent a shower of crystal drops scattering all over her as they shook their shaggy, dripping coats, barking and wagging their tails in uproarious joy. On this particular morning Clare felt more inclined to be quiet and think, but the dogs would not have it so; they knew what was best for their young mistress, and they insisted on their usual sport. Breathless and laughing she pulled up at last against a stile, and, leaning her arms on it, looked out at the ridge of gorse and heather that stretched below. A voice behind her startled her from her dreams. "You are out early. And how much you seem to enjoy life. I quite envied you that last run with the dogs—it was splendid!" Clare looked round surprised. She was always alone as a rule, always unmolested on her excur- sions, but here was a man, a stranger, actually daring to address her. He was tall, alert, clean- shaven, strong-jawed, with refined features, and dressed in the simplest of country garb. His hair was brown and rather thin on the top, his voice kindly, and in his grey eyes lay a kind of quizzical laughter—altogether an interesting kind of personage and decidedly a man of education. "I am an early riser like you," he said, noting her surprise, "and—an artist." He added this as an after-thought, as though to be an artist accounted for any kind Of eccentricity. Clare remained silent. After that one look of surprise she turned her head and continued to admire the landscape. "This is my favourite bit," he said, nothing daunted by her silenee. "See how prettily the ground slopes, and the broken bit of foreground with that clump of gorse is exquisite. Here, lying in the bracken, I can smoke and dream for hours." "Have yell been here long ? " (Mare asked, melting under the influence of his cheery talk. "A week," he said; "and I have seen you every day." "Indeed!. How interesting." A little note of contempt crept into her voice. "It was. You seem to have learnt the secret of existence—the joy of living." " knd you? You are an artists—don't artists -essw the joy of living." 'I was not always an artist. I have been a slave—a slave to uncongenial work. I have gone down into the gehenna of the struggle for life; been ground in the mill of hurry, of strife; lived in the grime of cities; suffered under the despair- ing race for bread." "And now you are going to live always in the country and paint beautiful pictures ? " "I think so—l hope so. Toil makes a man feel old. I shall take lessons of youth and , happiness from you." I "That is very nice," Clare said, kindly, "But lam -not always happy. Just now I only ran with my dogs to try and forget." I "You?" He looked at her in amazement. Her , brown eyes were deep and glowing; the sun made streaks of gold in her hair, and a delicate rose flush lay on her check. She was the embodiment of youth and health. I "Yes. Just as you are planning to pass your days here for the future I am probably going away." "I hope not." He spoke with real concern. ; Clare lauehed. a joyous. airlish laueh. TO STOUT PEOPLE.

Dr. Cassell's Tablets can be taken with as much benefit by stout people as thin. Excess of fat is due to defective assimila- tion. The nutrient properties of the food taken, instead of being perfectly absorbed, run to waste and make fat. Dr. Cassell's Tablets ensure a perfect natural, and healthy assimilation. causing superfluous fat to be absorbed and converted into sound, healthy flesh.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
IThe motion was approved, and the Secretary was , instructed to forward a copy to the Secretary of the Conference at Bristol. THE WORK OF THE CHURCHES. Mr. BRIMSLOW read a summary of reports on the work 'of the various churches.

Friars Green Church, Warrington, reported that the question of the incorporation had occupied much of their attention, and they were proud to think that the result had been to save their own and help in some degree in saving other churches from what they thought was detrimental to their highest and best interests. Their friends who differed from them had got what they wanted, plus the fetters of company laws, whereas they had got their liberty, which was worth far more than it had cost them. Incorporation had absorbed so much of their energies and otherwise obstructed true spiritual progress for so many months, they trusted it would receive decent burial at the forthcoming Conference, in a grave deep enough to hold it with all the unchristian thoughts and feelings which it had engendered. During the year their services had been fairly well attended. Their Sunday sehools were in every way growing, and they were now considering the question of large exten- sions to both their schools. Their C.E. Societies, senior and junior, continued in a healthy condition, but the weekly fellowship meeting was not in such a vigorous condition as they would wish.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
GENER.LL BUSINESS.

It was resolved that the General Committee be empowered to enter into negotiations with regard to an adjustment of the various funds of the committee as between this conference and the Incorporated Association.

It was also decided to publish a magazine for the members of the church.

It was decided to send a resolution to strengthen the hands of those who were appealing to Parliament to deal in the coming session with the enfranchisement of leasehold places of worship and schools, and the acquisi- tion of sites for such buildings. _ _ __ _ ____ _

Messrs. Houghton (Wigan),-Beesley (Prescot), Evans (Warrington), Isterling (Liverpool), Dodd (Liverpool), Marsh (Lovvton), Brimelow (Bolton), Allen (Platt Bridge), Howarth (Bolton), Bottomley (Batley), Kendrick (St. Helens), P. Gerrard (Lowton), R. Brimelaw (Southport), Scragg (Friars Green), S. Dale (Warrington), W. Wright and R. Heaton (Warrington) were elected to form the General Committee. It was decided to hold the next conference at Bolton, THE TEMPERANCE QUESTION.

On the proposition of Mr. HOLT (Bolton). seconded by Mr. HOUGHTON (Wigan), it was resolved "That the meeting expresses its satisfaction at the assurance made by the Prime Minister on behalf of the Government that an effective measure of temperance reform will have a prominent place in the legislative programme of the next session of Parliament."

===== 0002601_19070622_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PEACE CONFERENCE.

Mr. WRIGHT (Warrington) moved, and it was agreed "That this meeting learns with satisfaction of the assembly of the Conference at The Hague, and earnestly prays that the blessing of God may rest upon its efforts to promote the 'cause of international peace."

===== 0002601_19070622_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CONGO ATROCITIES.

It was resolved :—" That this meeting learns with horror of the atrocities and misrule on the Congo and presses the Government at once to take steps to end this blot on Christian civilization." EDUCATION AND POPULAR CONTROL.

On the proposition of Mr. E. BRIMBLOW, seconded by Mr. GERRARD, it was decided :—" That this assembly learns with satisfaction that the Government intends to introduce a comprehensive Education Bill next session of Parliament that shall give full popular con- trol over State-aided schools. We also urge that there sball be a greatly increased provision of colleges where teachers can be trained and that these colleges should be under the control of properly elected authorities. We farther desire to call the attention of the Education Minister to the fact that the Cowper Temple clause is still the law of the land, and to urge its loyal and vigor- ous enforcement by the Board of Education." A vote of thanks was passed to the friends at Priam Green for their kind hospitality to the delegates during the conference.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
EVENING MEETING.

There was a large attendance at a public meeting which was held in the evening, Mr.Rodson, Warrington, presiding. Mr. GERRARD, Lowton Common, delivered a power- ful address, urging upon all the recognition of their fellowship with Christ as an inspiration for the work of the furtnre.

Mr. H. Bitimmow urged that the truer end of Christianity was not to get men into Heaven but to get Heaven into men. Christianity was the only thing which would solve all the social problems of the day and it would bring about that golden age when men would cease to preach about living for their neighbours and would start. doing it. He would plead for their earnest effort in the cause of Christianity. Let them stop talking and set to work. The Gospel of Christianity was the only Gospel which harmonised with men's needs but it had not been given a fair chance. Let the world give it a fair trial and it would do all that was claimed for it.

Other interesting addresses were delivered and the meeting was very successful. A number of hymns were sung and anthems were ably rendered by the choir.
