"Mr. Andrews ! " "Mis Findon ! " Clare staggered back; there before her, a little paler and thinner than of yore, sad and expectant, but otherwise unchanged, stood the artist. "Why—oh! why have you done this ? " she said. "It was cruel."

Mr. Andrews placed a chair for her. She sat down unconsciously, for she could scarcely stand. , "Why cruel ? " he asked. "At first I meant no v.iystery, and afterwards you said such sharp and cutting things of your landlord that I did not dare!"

"And so you just allowed us te, be turned out," said Clare, bitterly. "That was your revenge." She was thoroughly unnerved by the surprise; she was trying to readjust her emotions to reconcile her liking for the artist with her hared for the millionaire.

"You are angry." He stood a little way off and looked at her earnestly. "And no wonder. It was wrong I know. I wanted to write, but I was afraid. Our companionship had been so pleasant I wished you to think well of me. I was a coward. Forgive me." "But why do you need our poor little house? Why were you so hard—you who have every- thing ? " She looked reproachfully round the quiet room, with the fine old oil portrait set into the big overmantel, the deep, soft pile of the Turkey carpet, the perfectly-appointed writing- table and chairs—all the paraphernalia of a gentleman's room. "Ah!" he replied, "you will never understand. I told you I had not always been an artist. I am a worker. I was born in poverty and bred up in toil, in all the sordid struggle of business. As a small boy I worked for myself and my mother; when I grew to be a man I slaved like a horse. I had no holidays, no pleasures, no time for things bright and beautiful—for art, for music; and all these years I have wanted— wanted. I said to myself that if ever I got to the top of the tree I would lead a different life —would know something of God's beautiful world and of all the great and noble things man's intellect has created. My dream mine true. I succeeded. lam rich. I bought this place. I thought to settle down here and find happiness— And then I met you." "I still don't understand."

"I told you you wouldn't. I had never spoken to a -sweet English girl before, you see; I knew no woman but my mother, and I—" "Well ? " she said, encouragingly. This talk was very different from what she had expected. "Well, I suppose I am younger really than I look. There comes a time to all when youth and nature must have its way, and then—well then—l loved you." Clare half rose.

"No, don't go; it is the last time we shall talk together. I have offended you past redemption, but stay just one more little minute." He stopped and took a long breath, panting.

l'are sat down. Thu s man fiseinatecr while he repelled her. He was strong at least, and she adored strength. "You have been very cruel," she said, thought- fully. "No, no! I struggled—you will never know how much. When a man finds the woman he has dreamt of and waited for all his life, and knows he can never win her, it is a bitter experi- ence."

"Still you would have let us go!" "No indeed. My lawyer has written you to-day, and sent you a new lease." e may stay?" A new note of triumphant ioy crept into Clare's voice. "Of course. Did you really think otherwise? Was that why you came to-day—to plead with the hard, vulgar millionaire? Ah I see." He struck his fist violently against the table. "Since I have met you, Miss Findon, I have cursed my riches ! "

"No, no; you will make an admirable use of them. I retract what I said ; when a rich man is good, he is better than a poor man—he has over- come more temptation. Pve been envious and uncharitable, and you have read me a lesson." A queer little break came in her throat. "Good- bye, and thank you." She held out her hand. Mr. Andrews gripped it with a warm, kindly pressure. "I must tell you, Miss Findon," he said, more lightly, "I never intended to put my coachman into your pretty house, and turn your garden into stables. I'm not such a vandal as that. I wanted the place as a dower-house for my mother —the poor old lady fancied it, and my life has always been given up to her." "Forgive me—" Clare turned and moved unsteadily towards the door. He sprang after her impetuously. "Miss Findon—Clare—the one unutterably bright, dear girl I've met—will you ever speak to me again ? " He asked the question with a certain boyish- ness and timidity. "Of course." Clare smiled, but her eyes were -"it with tears.

You will come again, and we may meet and talk together? Oh! thank you." Clare rushed home, back through the blinding sunshine, down the avenue, along the village street. She tore open the door of her mother's room and flung herself on her knees at her feet, burying her face in her lap; then between sobs and smiles she cried out: "Mother, it is all right; Lhave seen him. We are going to stay—stay— stay " "You've seen the millionaire, child; and what is he like, is he very vulgar?" "No." Clare smiled, a little, inscrutable, tender smile. "No, he is quite nice." "That's capital. There now, • let us put up the pictures again, and get out the books and the china—those shelves do look so bare—and make ourselves comfortable once more."

"The dogs need not go—oh, you dear, dear dogs ! " Clare bent down and kissed the warm muzzles of her pets, that kept boldly poking their noses into her hands and giving vent to short barks of agitated delight. "You dear, faithful friends, you know all about it—you know lam happy, so very, very happy!" Clare held out hez arms, and the dogs sprang into them. MIR END.]

SLUM CHILDREN

In narrow streets,all dank and vile, We see the homes of want and woe; Where warming sunbeams never smile, And healthful breezes never blow. There children born to toil and spin, Should Providence prolong their years; Their dreary round of life begin, • Entosed to suffering, want and Wax& In raggedness, all lank and lean, With sunken eyes, and faces pale; They look like spectres on the scene, To vouch a melancholy tale. The sounds of traffic, want and strife, And wand'ring minstrel's doleful song, Make up the music of their life, However brief, however long. The buttercup and blossomed thorn, The woodland songs and wild bee's hum, The waving grass and yellow corn, Know not the children of the slum. Their play-ground is the slimy street, , But toyless are their games, and few; For cobbles wound their naked feet, And chronic hunger pinches too. Deprived of all their Maker gave, Their dreary, hopeless way they tread; Their only goal perchance a grave— A rest among the nameless dead. And all this in a Christian land Unpitied goes from day to day; Where riches swell on every hand, And thoughtless folly loves to play. - J. H.. W. CLINDINNING. -

June, 1907

WOMAN HELPLESS WITH ECZEMA

Suffered Untold Agony for Three Years—Had to be Washed, Dressed, and Fed Like a Baby— Was In Doctor's Care and in infirmary but Could Not Get Cured.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
CUTICURA CURED HER IN THREE MONTHS

"I had eczema for three years Ind I was under doctor's treatment the whole of the time, also an infirmary, and I could not get cured. It would get better fGr a day or two, but would break out again worse than ever. At times it was so bad I had to be washed and dressed and even fed like a baby. It was only my hands and arms that were affected, but I suffered untold agony, and I got so low I was afraid to be left alone, and I had quite despaired of getting better, but at last I tried Cuticura. I used three boxes of Cuticura Ointment, and three bottles of Cuticura Pills, and in a month I was cured. That was twelve months ago, and I have seen no signs of it returning. I always keep a bottle of Cuticura Pills in the house. They do me more good than anything else, and I never use any other than Cuticura Soap. Mr. HALDANE has carried MR. HALDANE'S the third reading of his SUCCESS. Territorial • and Reserve Forces Bill against the vote of a curiously-mixed Opposition. On Tues.. day the Nationalists and Labour men were voting against each other on a matter on which the Irish members felt deeply, because Mr. PETE CURRAN'S chances have been greatly reduced at Jarrow through the appearance of a Nationalist candidate in the field. On Wednesday, however, the two parties below the gangway on the left-hand of the Speaker, buried the hatchet for the time being, and coalesced in opposition to Mr. HALDANE. There were Liberals opposed to the Bill, with Sir CHARLES DILKE at the head of them, and there were Conservatives pro nouncedly in its favour, amongst them being Mr GEORGE WYNDHAM. Of COUTIO, Mr. ARNOLD FORSTER remained hostile up to the last. Mr. HALDANE can never have thought with any hope- fulness of conciliating that right hon. gentleman, whose bitterness of spirit is a constantly operating factor, and destroys :whatever influence he might exercise if there were in his disposition a little more sweetness and light. The House of Commons has in the main treated the measure as if it wore one to be left to the experts. In the later stages there have been vast areas of empty green benches. That there was always a good supply of members in attendance became evident whenever a count was moved, but for hour after hour the discussions seem to have been carried on by members much fewer than the number which is recognised as constituting a House. We are not disposed to complain of this circumstance, however, seeing that the

===== 0002601_19070622_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
-'lt

majority of members cannot possibly possess a te2hnical knowledge of the :subject, and the Inatters:under discussion were to la large extent technical. Uninformed criticism: is much worse than a petulantly hostile criticism in such a case. Disagreement with the War Minister on hie:own side:has been concerned, not so much withlthe details of his scheme as with the expenditure Which it must involve. Mr. HALDANE has, in fact, disappointed the economists. As for the bulk of members of the House of Commons, they nave felt that they 'might safely leave the matter in the hands of the:SzeRBTARY 0311 WAR, Be has emerged from the severe trial of conducting the Bill through the IHouse with a greatly enhanced reputation. He is alMinister who spares no pains. He has a firm grasp of affairs, and he knows his subject so thoroughly that he was never taken unawares orlound to be at a loss, even by the experts. He is perceived 'to be a man with an enormous capacity for work, with a frame that work does not tire, end with a mind that is always on the alert. With his other great qualities, be combines extreme navity of manner. Criticism never m)ves him to anger, but only broadens his smile and increases bus willingness to explain. His scheme may be this or that; it cannot possibly be worse than the schemes of his predecessors and there is good reason to hope that it will turn out to be much better. It has yet to be tested by time. In the Meantime, however, Mr. HALDANE has shown himself to be both a great Parliamentatian and a strong administrator. As a personal success, the carrying of his Bill through its third reading must have realised his expectations,however highly they may have been placed.

There are Traders and Non- MINISTERS AND Traders even in ParliATnBnt, CONTRACTS. and a point was raised in the House of Commons on Thursday, which is of particular interest to Warrington people. Mr. Chamberlain refused to admit either the accuracy of the statement or the logic of the con- clusion. The same point has been emphasized and taborated by the Non-Traders in Warrington,and l‘as been met with the same perverse obtuseness. " is not the small shareholder in the large company who i 3 condemned as a trader, but the entleman with a directorship or controlling tirnence in a small or private company. The dis- `inction could not be much clearer to the average 1113hiased mind. After all, the Bible has not THE BIBLE IN THE been banished from the SCHOOLS. schools as a result of the I Government's Education Bill. Time and reflection seem to have brought all sects to a more reasonable frame of mind, the Churchmen in particular displaying now a spirit nt sweet reasonableness altogether foreign to their previous attitude on the question of religious I teaching. A:syllabus defining the Bible instruction and religious observances in Warrington Council 135h0015 has this week been almost unanimously adopted by the Ilducation Committee. In reality it amounts to the undenominational teaching 'Which Churchmen declared they would never accept. The teachers are" to read the Bible and give explanations and instructions therefrom in „ ‘an principles of Christian religion and morality as are suited to the capacities of the children." One 13r two members appeared to think that the 'exPlanations would be bound to take on a denomi- national hue in accordance with the beliefs the individual teachers, and that diffi- eu!ties would arise in consequence but, we think with Mr. Upson, that the teachers can be trusted to exercise a wise discretion in the matter, and avoid any religious difficulty whateve7. The aYllabus strikes the mean between sectarian and ascular teaching, and proves that a halfway course „ean be devised if all sects, and particularly the thurchm en, are prepared to concede something ni reason in order to keep the Bible in the schools. Canon Stevenson,—than whom there is no stauncher adherent of the Church and State—has 11,150 faith in the teachers and in the syllabus. "The Teachers," he said "valued the privilege of !aeing able to give such lessons as might I,nculcate in the children a higher sense of nwty and some elementary knowledge of the great principles of the Christian religion, es well as instil Into them some love for the Lord Jesus Christ." Ye further admitted the value of religious teach- ing and the wisdom of preserving it even in an nndenominational form. The difficulties of the question have apparently been magnified, and we I,ltre hopeful—indeed confident—that the problem la for the time being solved. Any recurrence of sectarian strife can only have one end—secular teaching pure and simple, which to Churchmen and Catholics at any rate is a very real bogey.

the weather will he kinder, they will maintain their reputation on this occasion. 5'41.10 Lord Robert Cecil is much A PARLIAMENTARY WO earnest a Parliament- FREAK arian to indulge in the foolish pastime of practical joking. Yet the notice which he gave to the Speaker of the House of Commons had much the appearance of an ill-timed flight of humour. As all the world knows, the Government intends next Monday to propose a resolution dealing with the mlmentous question of the House of Lords. It is the most important business of the session. All parties are waiting for the great pronouncement, with which is to be started a campaign which may end Heaven alone knows where. No one, to judge from the reports of speeches on either hand, wishes to postpone the debate or to shirk the issue. It is at this moment that Lord Robert, the scion of a great family of Parliamentarians, chooses to demonstrate the absurdity of the rules by which the House of Commons is regulated. The laws of the most grandmotherly assembly in Europe provide that no motion shall be made which will anticipate a matter already appointed for discussion. Lord Robert has, therefore, given notice of his intention to ask leave of the House to bring in a bill dealing with precisely the same subject as the Premier's carefully-elaborated resolution. The effect of this will be to render the resolution itself out of order. It is this object which Lord Robert wishes to achieve, not for the purpose of burking the House of Lords discussion,but to demonstrate the urgent necessity of a reform in the rules of the House which shall avoid so absurd a position being possible. Fortunately for the equanimity of the House, Lord Robert withdrew his notice yesterday. The object-lesson ought not to be with- out its advantages, for there may be occasions when the power to obstruct in the manner demonstrated may have a very irritating effect, and occasion much Parliamentary inconvenience. The possibilities or the situation were realised, it will be remembered, to some purpose recently, when the promoters of a motion dealing with the Woman's Suffrage question found themselves stopped by a similar process. The Government has already promised to take the whole question of" blocking motions" into consideration, and this action on the part of a member of the Opposi- tion should settle its determination to fulfil the pledge. --1

It seems a singular thing THE CHEERFULNESS that a man should devote OF MARK TWAIN. himself to making cheerful- ness a science. One would have thought that everybody knew how to be cheerful, but one of the comic things of life is to see how seriously some people take it. In this world one wants little more than food and a light suit like Mr. Clemens's. To call on every man for evidence of his indebtedness to Mark Twain would make a strange assize. One would go back to his first reading of " A Tramp Abroad" and "The Innocents Abroad," and perceive that the indelatedness ranged from "The Jumping Frog "—it is now; alas forty years since Jim Smiley filled it up with buckshot —to the new issue of the "North American , Review," to say nothing of the morning paper. Mark Twain is an artist as well as a humorist— witness the fact that every shot tells, and that seine of his descriptions in "More Tramps Abroad" (now about ten years old) linger in the memory still. Mark Twaiu—and there is more than humour in the advertisement of the fact—has reduced the question of rest to an exact science, and that is no doubt the secret of the fact that the old gaiety holds to the end. This is one of the parables of the prophet to this age. Mark Twain is as bright, as unexpected, and as daring as ever. He is still as mirth-provoking. Mr. Clemens must have indigestion sometimes, like other people, but his humour merely declines to sarcasm. Whatever else is to be said of Mr. Clemens, he has Anglat-Saxon grit and enterprise. The "Tom Sawyer "life, he told Mr. Kipling, wag "all the boy I have known or recollect " ; and that is highly significant. Some rough handling has gone to the polishing of the diamond. It is a , great boon, this invincible cheerfulness, but that a , man should set himself to get it, rather than to get some other thing in the world, must ever seem an amazing thing to this generation. Therein Mr. Clemens is a prophet.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The liveliest selection at Crosfield's last week was the quick-step " Silly Kate." No bricks please ! Save them for re-building. * * * The painful news is to hand that because of the glut of new potatoes the Jersey farmers are faced with ruin. Isn't it always the way? As soon as the poor consumer is able to purchase luxuries at 6, moderate price somebody howls that he is being ruined. * * *

The total wealth of the United Kingdom being £11,500,000,000, 5,009,000 people possesses £10,900,000,000, and 39,000,000 people possess £600,000,000. About 95 per cent, of the entire wealth of the United Kingdom is owned by about one-ninth of its population. o 0 elt Crosfield's Band is carrying all before it in the various competitions, and it is a question which approaches nearest" Perfection "—the music or the soap. There seems to be a most happy combination of both at Bank Quay. Which of the two the firm will shortly be most famous for we should not like to predict.

One of the " features " of the Pte at Hill Cliffe is the sight of Mr. Henry Greenall in the role of barman. There he stands in the refreshment tent, sleeves rolled up, and apron on, ready to serve all and sundry with liquid refreshment. We don't blame Mr. Greenall we admire him for his self-sacrifice and indifference to appearances. There are not a great many gentlemen of means who would care to have their private park cut up for the purposes of a bazaar, and who would further demonstrate the whole-heartedness of their enthusiasm by serving in the bar. *

Staid as they may be as a deliberative assembly the Warrington Guardians of the Poor thoroughly enjoy themselves when out for the day. On Thursday they held their annual trip and visited the Dukeries. One of the large party which went, thoughtfully provided himself with a plentiful supply of strawberries, but these he was not fated to enjoy as they were promptly commandeered and disposed of by his colleagus. Singularly enough the weather at Sherwood Forest was in direct contrast to that which prevailed in War- rington, the sun shining brilliantly mosVf the day. * * * The Lords have been discussing a Bill to permit women to sit on public bodies. "Then, thank good- ness," writes an attenuated married male, "that the bodies of husbands can in no sense be regarded as public."—Lord Crewe, in moving the second reading of the above measure, laid stress upon the extreme usefulness of women in taking care of lunatics. Does his lordship seriously suggest that this fact readers a woman peculiarly eligible for election to County Councils ?—" London Opinion."

Mr. John Burns, who in odd moments is writing his reminiscences, has been spcitking of the extra- ordinary mortality among the once famous band of Labour leaders. Nearly all of them," he said, "are either dead or shattered in health except myself —and I have always had the constitution of an ox. William Morris, Tom Af`Carthy, and Eleanor Marx- Aveling are dead; Ben Tillett, H. H. Champion, and Keir Hardie are ill; and Cunningham° Graham and Bernard Shaw have long ceased to participate actively in i the movement.' It s the strenuous life !" * * * The most useful thing the long run.—Breath.— , " Globe."

The Examiner, Saturday, June 22, 1907

wisdom is humble that he knows no more.—COWPER. Any right-minded girl would sooner wear a new hat that didn't suit her than an old hat that did.—Fa RICHARDSON. We have no more right to consume happiness with- out producing it than to consume wealth without pro- ducing it. —GEORGE BERNARD SHAW. 1 0 An apparently clodpated countryman offered a ! Quaker hatter fifteen shillings for a new beaver. " As I live," said the Quaker, " I cannot afford to let thee have it at that price." "As you live, very likely ' not," replied the would-be purchaser, "but live more moderately, and be hanged to you ! " " Friend,"• said the Quaker, "thou mayst h..ve this hat for nothing."—" T.P.'s Weekly." * * Jarrow. just now the most interesting spot politi- cally in England, owing to the fight of four candidates for one seat, is (says a "Manchester Guardian "corres- pondent) one of the most sternly industrial of place,. It is a collection of huge cranes, chimneys, and 1(.,v browed cottages. It is one of the few towns whe; e these one-decker dwellings can be seen. Black ad uninviting as it is, it contains, hidden in a wilderness of colliers' houses, one of the most sacred historical spots in the country. This is the little hill on which stand a few scraps of ancient wall surrounding the church—remnants of Jarrow monastery, founded in the seventh century. Here lived and worked, as student, monk, scholar, and historian, the Venerable Bede, "candle of the English Church." Bede refused to leave his cell in Jarrow even to see the Pope ; here he toiled at his books, and laboured at winnowing and threshing the monastic corn, giving milk to the lambs and calves and taking his share in the garden and bakehouse. He died here just as he finished the last words of his translation of the Gospels, and was buried in the porch of the church, where, almost until Norman times, crowds of pilgrims came to visit his tomb.

Members of Warrington and Crosfield's Corps of! the St. John Ambulance Brigade were inspected on Wednesday evening by Mr. W. P. Woodcock, chief superintendent of No. 4 (North-Western) District. Some 60 men mustered, and the officers on parade were: Captain Waring, chief superintendent of Warrington ; Mr. S. Balslry, superintendent ; Mr. F. Taylor, second officer ; Mr. Studer, superintendent Crosfield's Corps. Mr. F. Rose, first officer. The nursing division, under the command of Miss Hackett, were also inspected. The men performed several evolutions with considerable smartness, and were complimented by Mr. Woodcock, who said that in his report to the Deputy Commissioner it would give him pleasure to say that they had given satisfaction. He would like to call their attention to the military home hospital reserve,formed for the purpose of assisting the army corps on active service. He hoped many names would be enrolled from Warrington and that those who entered their names would be prepared to carry out the obligations. The Warrington Union Guardians and officials on Thursday held their twientieth annual excursion, Sherwood Forest and the Dukeries being visited. , Rain fell early in the morning and as a consequence the attendance was not so large as usual but those who ' braved the elements spent a most enjoyable day, the weather in the Dukeries being brilliantly fine. Welbeck was left at 4.40 and Worksop again reached half an hour later. Caretakers of Warrington elementary schools attended in large numbers, a meeting held on Saturday, at which an address on the formation of a federation for Warrington and district was delivered by Mr. Oliver, of Liverpool (president of the Liverpool branch and vice-president of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Federation of Care- takers). Particular stress was laid on the objects—to improve the present and prospective condition of its members, to regulate, as far as possible, the relation between caretakers and employers, and to encourage social and fraternal intercourse amongst its members and their interest and well-being. Mr. Curtis (secretary Burnley and District Branch), Mr Kemp (Bradford Branch), and Mr. Williams (Liverpool), also spoke. It was unani- mously decided to form a branch in Warrington, and to affiliate with the Lancashire and Yorkshire Federa- tion.

WALKING DAY. PREPARATIONS FOR NEXT FRIDAY'S

FESTIVAL.

Friday next is Walking Day, and for this day of days in the lives of the children, great preparations are being made. The all-absorbing question at present is: Will the weather be fine? The unseasonable conditions of the past week have given some anxiety, for a wet Walking Day is a great calamity. How- ever, the townspeople are looking forward hopefully to a great improvement. Only given sunshine, and the Walking Day of 1907 will, we are sure, be quite as successful as any which the old town has seen. for many weeks school officials have been busy pushing forward the arrangements, and there will be nothingl wanting on their part to ensure a happy day. From an early hour the streets will present animated scenes. Then the processions gay with banners and headed by bands discoursing lively airs, will move on their wgy to the lawn facing the Town Hall—the place of assembly of the Church of England and Noncon- formist schools. Promptly at ten o'clock the signal will be given for the Blue Coat School children —who always have the place of honour —to lead off the pro- cession through the centre arch. The schools will leave in the following order :

(1) Blue Coat School and Band. - (2) Padgate Industrial School and Band. (3) Parish Church and St. Helen's Church, Holinfare. (4) St. George's Mission. (5) St. Clement's Mission. (6) St. Peter's School. (7) St. Paul's. (8) St. Ann's. (9) Holy Trinity. (10) St. Luke's. (11) Factory Yard Mission. (12) St. James's. (13) St. Barnabas' (14) Emmanuel Reformed Church. (15) Beckley-street. (16) Salvation Army and Band. (17) Ragged School and Drum mad Fife Band. The usual route Arpley-street, Wilson Patten- street and Bridge-street—will be followed. As far as can be gathered, the procession will not present many new features but will as in former years be mainly imposing as a demonstration of strength. In the afternoon thore will be an invasion of fields in all parts of the district, where the youngsters will be able to romp to their hearts' content. There will be the usual sports—which are certain to be contested with great keenness, and tea will he provided. If the day is fine, a happy time is assured. Following the example of previous years, most of the Nonconformist schools will content themselves with a procession along a short route of their own, their intention being to give the children a complete day's enjoyment in the country.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
ROMAN CATHOLIC PROCESSION.

The Roman Catholic procession is always the principal feature of Walking Day and Friday's pageant promises to excel in brilliance any previous enter- prises of the kind. Teachers and scholars alike are sparing no pains to ensure the complete success of the procession and in their efforts they have the loyal co-operation of the parents —and as a result the scene is one of the most beautiful imaginable and always lives long in the memories of those privileged to witness it. As usual the procession will take place immediately after the Church of England and Noncon- formist scholars have passed along the line of route. The display will be seen in its entirety in Bridge- street and Sankey-street, and the place of honour will be held by St. Mary's school. Large banners of , exquisite texture and workmanship will again be a magnificent feature. Theleading one will bear a copy of Murillo's famous picture "The Immaculate Con- • ception," whilst others will depict the Seven • Sacraments, and the great Saints of the Church.

NEW ART MASTER. Shipley Gentleman Appointat

At a meeting of the School Management Committee of the Warrinton Education Committee held yester- day tifternoon, Mr. Clarence Mawson, A.R.C.A., of Shipley, was appointed headmaster of the Warrington School of Art in succession to Mr. C. B. Aylward, who retires next month. Mr. Mawson commences his duties at the beginning of September. The remunera- tion offered is a fixed salary of £225 per annum together with a grant of 10s. for each paper in excess of 125 worked by the students of the school at the Board of Education's annual examinations, the total maximum salary not to exceed 300 per annum.

SWIMMING NOTE&

South Hill, Liverpool, was visited on Wednesday last, this being a League match. The result was Soutk Hill 5, Warrington 4. The referee was very unsatisfactory throughout the game and a protest has been laid before the League Committee on the grounds of inefficient referee and inaccurate time-keeping. The next match is away with St. Hsiang on Tuesday, June 2.5 th.

The Committee have arranged for a match to be played here on Thursday, July 4th, the visitors being Leigh. On this occasion the first round of the Works Squadron Competition will be swam off, the competing teams being Richmonds v. Co-operatine Stores and Pearson and Knowles v. Crosffelds. The following have byes in the first round : —P. Walker and Sons, Rylands, Monks Hall, Corporation, Post Office, and Sankey Wire Mills.

There should be some very interesting swimming between the clubs drawn in the first round, especially Pearson and Knowles v. Crostlelde, both these teams having won the Shield, Pearson and Knowles last year and Gros!'kids the previous year.

FREEDOM OF BOKOUGH PAWNED. That the freedom of a borough can be pawned was demonstrated in a case at Sunderland Police Court on Thursday. The defendanti'was George Cook. He had gone through the South African war, and on his return had received a war medal and the freedom of Sunder- land. He had pawned the medal and certificate of freedom. His wife made grave charges against him, and the Bench adjourned the case for three months to give them a chance of "making up.* CELL LOCK PICKED WITH BOOT-LACE. , George Thompson, a well-dressed man, was com- mitted for trial at Wilmslow on Thursday on a charge of burglary at Alderley. It was stated in court that while on remand he had actually managed to pick the lock of his cell by means of a bootlaoe, and was quietly 'walking out of the police station when he was seen and re-arrested.

Destructive Brother. Fred Archer, lodging in Rose and Crown-street, was charged at the Borough Police Court, on Tuesday morning, with wilfully committing damage at the dwelling-house of his brother, Roger Archer, 171, Bewsey-road. On Sunday night prisoner, who did not live with his brother, went to the house and demanded money for his lodgings. He was refused. He then picked up a grass sod and hurled it through the window. The Chairman (Dr. Fox) said they must protect the peaceful and law-abiding people from such rascals. A fine of 20s. and costs or one month was imposed. NORFOLK Sun's from 18/6—BANKs,Outfitter.—Advt. I RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RIVER MYSTERY NEAR WIDNES.

On Saturday the body of a well-dressed man was found in the river Mersey near the Old Marsh, Widnes, From all appearances the body had been in the water for some considerable time and was in an advanced stage of decomposition. There was nothing to dis- cover his identity in his pocket. He was about fifty years of age, height sft. 6iu., slight dark moustache, well built, every appearance of a clerk; wearing dark suit with light stripes, dark striped trousers, white cotton shirt, and laced boots. The following were found in his possession :—A pair of gold-rimmed spectacles in a leather case bearing the following inscription: Carty and Brodgton, London; also a pair of steel-rimmed spectacles, a black pocket knife,and one pearl.cuff link. GIRL'S FATAL SAIL.

At Widnes, on Sunday afternoon, some little girls went to play at Westbank Dock, and after being there some time, they got into a boat and floated about the dock. About five o'clock one of the girls, named Lawton, shouted, "The police are coming." The three girls made a dart for a dredger close by. When almost alongside, two of the girls jumped aboard, and the other, Sarah Roach, aged tkirteen years, daughter of James Roach, chemical labourer, of Pear-street,Widnes slipped and fell into the water. There was no one about .who could attempt a rescue, and the girl went down. Shortly after assistance was procured, and Thomas Hughes went into the water and recovered the body. Artificial respiration was tried for some time,but without avail. The body was conveyed to the mortuary.

WIDNES INQUESTS.—A SERIOUS COMPLAINT. picked ismirsacrAt t.kl esrwddta ahamseyuuuWpe upon oi Bodinnrnr ntie s nhetPgho b. oleuos ideTcyerhp eorvSefetsbraiaodtni deo:yuni enohorkvsanneedoryTw: been wnee asoemtdn inquests.aaynv in iwtdhhn eieeswhich a, Twtoheaners, many weeks, and has not been identified. The jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned." —A verdict of "Accidentally drowned " was returned after the inquiry into the death of Sarah Roach, aged thirteen years, daughter of James Roach, chemical labourer, Pear-street, who was drowned in the West Bank Dock on Sunday. Deceased and several other girls went to play there, and got into a boat, when one of them raised the cry the " Police are coming." A girl named Nickson and another named Lawton, who were also in the boat, got out on to a dredger; but the last one, Sarah Roach, slipped and fell into 12ft. of water. Thomas Hughes, a young man, stripped off his clothes and entered the water, recovering the body. Artificial respiration was resorted to without avail.— Mr. William Tracey, foreman of the jury, addressing the Coroner, made a serious complaint. of taking bodies in an advanced state of decomposition to the mortuary at the hospital. He went to visit the body, the subject of the first inquiry, on Monday, and the stench was something abominable. The matron and her staff had to live there, and the cooking for the staff and patients had to be done not eight yards away. It was also in the centre of a thronged population, and he feared illnecs would ensue with its repetition. The Coroner said he knew the hospital well, and was sur- prised at the state of things. He did not know bow Nurse Nutler lived about. It was quite illegal for a body of that description to be taken there. One kind of mortuary could not be used for the purpose of another, and it was a subject that ought to be brought before notice of the local authority. A Juror.—lt was brought up in November last. Mr. Poole (solicitor).— Possibly as an election cry.

ST. HELENS.

OLD MAN FATALLY BURNED. The Deputy-Coroner held an inquiry at the St. Helens Town Hall on Monday into the death of Richard Ward, aged seventy-two, a blacksmith, of Crossley-road. Owing to an affection of the heart he was not able to follow his employment. Latterly he had become childish, and, his housekeeper had to dress and wash him. On Wednesday afternoon he was seated on a chair near the fire, and his housekeeper, who was in the yard, hearing screams rushed into the house and found deceased in flames. He had evidently been lighting his pipe. The housekeeper poured water over him, and with assistance extinguished the flames. Dr. Graham attended him and he was removed to the Providence Hospital, where he died next day. A verdict of Accidental death" was returned.

SHOCKING COLLIERY ACCIDENT. YOUTH'S AWFUL DEATH. Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy county coroner, held an inquest on Monday on Samuel Yates, aged seventeen, of 108, Ormskirk-road, St. Helens, who was killed at the Ramford Colliery on Thursday, when his skull was badly fractured owing to a number of empty waggons running away. Yates was working with Samuel Rigby, who was working a mechanical cutter. Yates was raking coal away from the machine, and was between the cutter and two full waggons of coal. The empty waggons were sent down the inclineby a haulage engine tenter, and they attained a great speed, and, dashing into the full waggons smashed Yates's head against the machine.—John Smith, collier, of 26, Bartons-row,spoke to seeing deceased struck. He liberated him but death was immediate.—Thomas Rimmer, aged thirteen, said he was sending the boxes down the road. He had been , letting them down slowly, but he was told to send them faster, as the colliers were waiting for them. He did this and when he got the signal to stop he immediately pulled down the reversing lever and put on the air, but seeing the tubs were not stopping he jumped on the hand brake with both feet, but failed to stop the waggons before they ran into the full ones.—Mr. Matthews, Government inspector, said he thought the ' youth who was selected for that work should always be older and experienced.—The jury returned a verdict of 1" Accidental death." -

- - - NEW MISSION CHURCII. The foundation stone of St. Andrew's Mission Church at St. Helens was laid on Saturday afternoon by Mrs. Chavasse, who was accompanied by the Bishop of Liver- pool. The mission church is being built to meet the need of the populous district of Denton's Green, which is included in the parish of St. Helens. The cost will be about £2,700, which will be met by the appeal fund which the vicar of St. Helens, the Rev. Cyril Bardsley, started some time ago, and by which .£1,500 has been raised. The Rev. H. S. Broadbent, curate in charge, presided at the opening ceremony —Mrs. Chavasse was presented with a silver trowel by the architect (Mr. Biram) and a mallet by the con- tractor (Mr. (Mr. Litherland), after which the stone was laid " in the faith of Jesus Christ."—The Bishop, addressing the assembly, congratulated them on the foundation of 'that mission, for it was an evidence of spiritual life. Their spiritual life was starting in a fresh direction with the laying of that foundation-stono. He thanked 'God that there was was an epidemic of church building just at present. Billinge was beautifying its church. Haydock would be building a new one shortly, and at Abram, Widnes, and Crouton they were adding to their church accommodation. All he could say, as their bishop and friend, was that he hoped that epidemic would spread, and grow stronger and stronger. The more churches they had the better it would be for the people. They were building that church in a critical time. They were threatened on some sides with disestablishment and disendowment. Even if the worst came and it was disestablished, the Church would still go go and do good work. They were also told that they were going to be disendowed, but he reminded them that not a single clergyman in the diocese of Liverpool, nor the Bishop, received one halfpenny from the State, except as prison chaplain or workhouse chaplain. The Bishop and clergy were paid by the money which people in days gone by gave to the church in the same ways as they had given to that mission chapel. The State had the petwer to take that money away by Act of Parliament,but whether it would be right to take away the money he would leave them to judge. The Church had been more and more founded on the hearts of the people, and she could face the , future without fear. In spite of all that was said about disestablishment and disendowment they went forward building and rebuildipg churches,believing that nothing could hurt the Church which was founded and carried on in the name of Jesus. Votes of thanks closed the proceedings. -.Mb-

THE FINISHING TOUCH.

No tea table is quite complete without a good supply of that old-fashioned dainty—Currant Bread. Be sure to slice it very thin, as by this means the fruit is cut through and all the delicious flavour is drawn out. Such excellent Currant Bread is now being made by all enter- prising bakers, and its cost is so little above that of the ordinary loaf, that it is not worth the housewife's while to make it at home.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON

PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

The Rev. J. H. Rollason, Primitive Methodist minister, Earlestown, was summoned at Newton on Thursday last week for non-payment of Bs., Education rate. He did not appear, and the usual order was made

DEFRAUDING THE LONDON AND NORTH WESTERN RAILWAY.

At Warrington County Sessions on Wednesday, Mary Collier, of Earlestown,was fined 20s.and costs for travell- ing on the London and North-Western Railway from Widnes to Fiddlers Ferry without having previously paid her fare and with intent to avoid payment. It was stated that defendant joined the train at Widnes, and when passing through the ticket-collecting barrier at Warrington she offered the collector the fare from Fiddlers Ferry. Detective Coward spoke to seeing the woman join the train at Widnes. FORGEMEN'S PICNIC.

The forgemen connected with the London and North- Western Railway Company's Waggon Wo rks,Earles town had their annual picnic on Saturday,when they journeyed to Chester. A saloon was placed at their disposal and on arriving at Chester they made the Bowling Green Hotel their headquarters. After the Cathedral, Eaton Hall and other places had been visited they returned to the hotel, where an excellent tea awaited them, the usual toasts being given and thanks accorded to the managers of the trip. A few songs were sung and a very pleasant evening was spent, the party numbering about 30.

PECULIAR NEWTON CASE.

At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wed- nesday, a respectably-dressed man named Garrett Ronayne, of High-street. Newton-le-Willows, was charged with stealing a grey mare, ralli oar, and set of harness, valued at £5O, the property of his father, William Ronayne.—The father said he did not appear to prosecute, and denied giving information to the police that his horse and trap were stolen.—lnspector Innes said that the prisoner was arrested on suspicion of stealing the horse and trap, but escaped and got away 2i miles before being caught. He had previously offered to sell the horse and trap for £3o.—Prisoner was remanded to Newton Petty Sessions. His father went bail to the extent of £2O.

TFIE NEW MAGISTRATES.

The Burtonwood Parish Monthly says "The names of C. B. F. Borron, W. Ormand, W. Collingwood and E. T. Evans are among the best known in our immedi- ate neighbourhood. It is no surprise to learn that names so honourably known should have " appended to them. It is their right. We congratu- late these gentlemen on their promotion to the Bench which is always in need of some sane, merciful men, of sufficient courage to administer justice without partiality. Justices' justice, as Mr. Labouchere never tires of proving, is commonly erratic and, at times, is subject to strange halluciations. It has been said of long time that these hallucinations are due to party politics, or social bias, or, again, to crass ignorance and asinine stupidity. Our best wish for our new local , magistrates is that they may never find themselves I pilloried in the columns of "Truth." From what we know of them they run little risk of this ordeal. But whatever has become of the Labour magistrates? We looked for them but their names could nowhere be found! Are they not worthy to be numbered with the Great Unpaid ? "

===== 0002601_19070622_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
THEFT FRONT A PUBLIC-HOUSE.

John Hayes, of Ciolborne, was charged at Newton last Thursday with stealing 6s. 6d. and a spirit glass from the the Bull's Head, Lowton. He pleaded gailty, but said he remembered nothing about it.—Harriet Bates, licensee of the Bull's Head, said another gentle- man went to the bar and ordered drink. Soon after, hearing that the prisoner had been into the private bar, and noticing that a money box had been moved, she looked into the box and missed Gs. 6d. She taxed - him and said she would have him examined before he went out. He turned, saying she might examine his pockets,and as he turned one pocket for examination he took something out of his other pocket and passed it to his companion. It was intercepted and found to be the missing money, in the exact coins she knew to have been in the box. The spirit glass was in his jacket pocket.—Ernest Harrison, of Newton, who was in the house at the time, said he stopped the passage 'of the coins from the prisoner to the other man. Prisoner seemed sober.—P.C. Benson, who apprehended the prisoner, said Fe admitted the offence.—Prisoner admitted it in Court, but said he had had his head hurt in the pit, and he did not know what he doing. For the sake of his wife and children he asked the Bench to look over it; nothing of the kind should occur again.— Inspector Innes said the man had a very nice home, and nothing before was known against him.—The Chairman said the magistrates would give him a chance by merely binding him over as a first offender to come up for judgment if called on.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
MATRINIONIAL DIFFERENCES.

At Newton Petty Sessions last Thursday, George Makin, jun., Earlestown, was charged. by his wife with failing to maintain her and treating her with persistent cruelty.—Mr.?. S. Brian, of Hindley, appeared for the wife?and said she was not prepared to go on with the case because her husband had told her she need not bring her witnesses as the case would not go on. Con- sequently, her witnesses were not there, and he asked for an adjournment. —Mr. Knowles, for the defence, objected, being ready to go on. If the plaintiff chose to go to her witnesses and say the parties were going to settle the matter and they need not to go to Court, he did not agree to an adjournment. If the summons were diimissed the parties would make up their differences and be living together, the best of friends, within a week.—The Bench thought that if such an arrangement could be made it would be very much the best course, and they adjourned the case for the parties and their solicitors to confer together.—The case was accordingly adjourned for a fortnight, John Thompson, printer at Golborne paper staining works, was summoned by his wife Lilian Thompson, for persistent cruelty.—Mr. H. B. Rigby, of Leigh, appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Knowles defended. —The wife told a story of illtreatment, ending in her being kicked out of the house, on which she went home to her parents at Failsworth, near Bolton, and refused to go back. Her husband was all right when sober, but mad and violent when drunk.—Mr. Knowles represented that the plaintiff's story was a mass of exaggeration, and there would be no trouble between the parties if the plaintiff's father and mother would let them alone. —Defendant was sworn and said that was so. He denied having locked his wife out and nailed the door up against her, but admitted that. when under the Inllu3nce of drink he was a"a little bit crazy." He was willing to take his wife home.—The Chairman hoped the parties would live together, the husband promising to behave properly and the wife making allowances and taking care of her own temper.—Plaintiff: I have no temper— that's where it is.—The Chairman: We are not quite I sure of that. —Ultimately the summons was dismissed.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
DEPARTURE OF THE REV. C. L. STONE.

The members of the Legh-street Christian Endeavour Society on Monday evening bade farewell to the Rev. C. L. Stowe, the minister of the church, who is leaving shortly to join the Eccles circuit. The President (Mr. T. B. Parkes) said that that would be the last time they would have the presence of their honorary president, the Rev. 6. L. Stowe, who had taken a great interest in the Young People's Society. The founder of the society, the late Rev. Charles Finch, had worked hard for it, but be did not think if Mr. Stowe had been the founder he could have evinced a greater interest in it, and he had helped them greatly. He hoped that in his next circuit he would be well received, and hoped God's blessing would go with him and Mrs. Stowe.— Mr. Landor, the vice-president of the society, endorsed all that Mr. Parkes had said. He was sure that when Mr. Stowe left they would miss him very much.—The Rev. C. L. Stowe responded, and said he tried to inakeit a rule of his life that, whether praised or blamed, it would make no difference to his labours. He felt truly grateful for the kindly words spoken about him, which he knew were echoed by each Endeavourer present. He would give them a word of advice before he left them, and that was that if they would have their Endeavour succeed, they must work. The singing of" God be with you till we meet again" concluded the meeting.

ANOTHER FAREWELL GATHERING.

An interesting social gathering and concert took place at Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church on Thursday evening. There was a large and representative attendance of members of Legh-street and Ivnutsford- road Churches. The gathering was also made the occasion of saying a few words of farewell to the Rev. C. L. Stowe and Mrs. Stowe. An interesting programme of musical selections was submitted, songs being rendered by Miss A. Bridge, Miss L. Snelling and Mr. Osborne. Mr. Smith gave some capital examples of rapid sketching on the blackboard. Mr. R. BRIDGE, circuit steward, presided and addresses were delivered by the Revs. John Yonge and J. F. Matthews, and Messrs. J. Griffiths and J. Bissell. All the speakers referred in kindly terms to the work of Mr. Stowe in Warrington during the past three years, Mr. Tonge specially mentioning his co-operation with his brother ministers. The best of wishes were expressed for Mr. Stowe's future ministry and the strong hope was held out by more than one speaker that it would not be long before he was back in his old pulpit. The Rev. C. L. STOWE in reply said the kind words spoken would be an inspiration for the days that were to come and urged all the members and friends to believe that the best days for the Warrington circuit. were yet in store. He asked that the uniform kindness which had been shown to him throughout his stay might. be extended to his successor, the Rev. J. B. Buglass. Mr. Stowe preaches his farewell sermons on Sunday

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington accomplished a very smart performance on Saturday when they journeyed to Earlestown and defeated the champions on their own ground. The match was of considerable importance and ‘Varrington's victory will probably give them the championship of the %Vest Lancashire League—that is,of course,provided the team continues in the excellent form which it is at present showing. St. Helens are still top with an unbeaten record but as they have several stiff matches before them this record will possibly be spoiled before long.

WHITE CITY, MANCHESTER.

Hale's Tours of the World.

Hale's Tours of the World is an Exhibition, the intention of which is to educate the spectator, through the medium of moving and animated pictures in the picturesque life of the world. They make an enjoyable entertainment rendering the reading of books or the viewing of works of sculptors, artists, and writers on all subjects more easily comprehended and enjoyed in years to come. It is especially instructive to the untravelled and to the rising generation to see authenti- cated, genuine people, different nations and races in their characteristic costume before they have passed away and are left as legacies to the future only through history. The incidents and episodes shown have additional interest, as they have been taken by a staff of operators exclusively for the Hale's Tours of the World Company. Notwithstanding the tardiness of Summer, thousands of well pleased patrons have visited the White City, where mirth and merriment reign suprerne,since the Oates were thrown open to the public some few weeks ago—the principle features of the City of Pleasure, its it has been aptly termed, are now in good running order, and new attractions are being added from time to time. It is without doubt the most extra- ordinary as well as most beautiful place of its kind anywhere. One can fully appreciate the work done by Nianaging Director, J. Calvin Brown, who sustains the reputation made list him as a veritable " wizard." The triumphs of genius displayed are greatly to his credit.

SIXTEEN SUPPURATING ULCERS.

" One Mass of Corruption "—Leg Saved and Cured by Zam-Buk.

A poisoned and ulcerated leg, so full of corrupt wounds that amputation was imminent, was the legacy left by a cycling mishap to Mr. William Bennett, of Station Rill, Grampouni Road, Cornwall. To a" West Briton " reporter Mr. Bennett said :—" On August 23rd 1900, I had a side-slip from my bike which threw me violently into a ditch. Some passers-by found me and took me home. I was attended by two doctors, and then taken to the infirmary, where it was found that the injuries comprised a dislocated elbow,a broken shoulder a fractured hip, and a broken leg.

"I remained at the hospital for about eight weeks, and when I left I had to use crutches to get about the house. After some months of this a wound broke out, on my leg, and I had to go to the infirmary again,suffer- ing from severe ulceration. I was 'patched up' once more, but had not been at home again long when ulcers formed in several places, and ultimately I was confined to my bed with no less than sixteen discharging wounds. I was nearly driven out of my mind with the pain, and the leg was simply one mass of corruption. "All sorts of medicines, ordinary ointments and lotions, herbs, &c., were tried, but with no good effect. Everyone thought I should lose my leg. As a last resort I was persuaded to try Zatn-Buk, and within half- an-hour of the first application I fell sound asleep. "Of course, T continued with Zam-Buk, and now, after using about half-s-dozen boxes, ray leg is as sound as sound can be. All the wounds filed up with sound, firm flesh, and Zam-Buk covered them with new healthy skin."

Zam-Buk is natural and neyer-failing—especially when helped by ZAM-BEJK MEDICINAL SOAP, which is unique, cooling, comforting, and cleansing in all kinds of skin troubles.

Local Athletic Successes.

In the mile race at the Prescot Wire Works Sports on Saturda,y,the first prize was won by G. Watmough, Warrington AC., who was two yards in advance of T. Clarke, Sephton, from the 140 yards mark. At the Cheshire Lines sports at Lostock Gralam, J. Gibson won the first prize for the 220 yards race and the second in the 100 yards race. In the 150 yards race youths, E. Horrocks (10) was placed second. H, Jepson was second in the mile, whilst W. Drury was third in the quarter-mile.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
SECOND DIVISION.

--..,..... Prescot Warrington Newton St. Helens St. Helens Recs. 6 2 4 0 4 Stanley

Warrington's match on Saturday was somewhat singular in that Earlestown, who are generally regarded as a very strong batting side, failed altogether before the deliveries of Creswell and Callon and were all dis- missed for 59. Creswell had the splendid average of three runs per wicket for each of the six victims he claimed, whilst Callon's figures were 4 for 38.

Warrington opened their innings well and 15 runs were up before the first wicket fell, Richardson being lbw for six. Then J. Callon and Wright became associsted and proceeded to bang the bowling. about. Rain threatened continually and in their eagerness to get runs both men took considerable risks. As events proved, the forcing game paid handsomely as 81 had been knocked up in quick time when the weather inter- vened and the game had to be abandoned, Warrington claiming a brilliint victory. To Wright in particular was this success largely due as when the game ended he was 30 not out. Callan had scored 24 and at the close was also unbeaten.

Warrington at the present time have a very strong team and it is hoped that they can be kept together for the rest of the season. Connor's return to the field is very welcome and it is to be hoped that he will display some of his old form. To-day the team meet St Helens Recs. on the Arpley ground.

Orford Barracks met with their first reverse in the Warrington and District League on Saturday when they were defeated by Cheshire Lines. The result was due largely to an error of judgment as with 90 runs for 8 wickets, the Orford captain closed his innings. Out of this total Johnson claimed 48 not out. Cheshire Lines lost two men very cheaply but Duckworth and Hill made a stand. The former player quickly got his eye in and hit up the runs at a rapid rate and without further loss the Barracks score was passed before time was called. Duckworth came out with the splendid total of 61 not out, Hill being 23, and practically won the match by scoring against time,

A new batting record has been created In the Works Competition, as against Cheshire Lines on Tuesday Thompson for P. &K. knocked up 62. This was not the only record of the match, as Cheshire Lines were promptly tumbled out for 5, three being extras ! This singular failure is difficult to explain, as the team are by no means inexperienced. %V. Bennett could probably advance a reason, as he took seven wickets for one run and bowled magnificently. -

Orford secured a victory over Caley on Saturday by 8 runs and four wickets. Singularly enough the top score of the match (18) was made for the losers by Millet% but the Orford men all met with success.

A. Holbrook and A. Turton bowled well for Arpley Athletic against Stockton Heath Y.M. Club on Satur- day, taking respectively 5 for 11 and 5 for 8. Asa result the Stockton Heath score was only 22, and this the Athletics passed with two wickets in hand.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
Swimming.

Warrington Polo team are this season a force to be reckoned with and their return to form will doubtless lead to a desirable increase in the interest in the swimming world. On Tuesday there was a very large attendance at the baths to witness the fixture with Bacup and the spectators were treated to a first class exhibition by the local men: The match was one of considerable importance as it was the first round of the Lancashire County Cup and Warrington made no mistake. Bacup were an unknown quantity but as events proved they met more than their match. In

speed they could hardly compete with the wirepuller whilst in the matter of shooting they were awfully weak. This was Warrington's strong point and at long range they subjected the Bacup goalkeeper to a hot fusilade, beating him no fewer than eight times despite the fact that he played a good game.

The reserve team lacked training and in a friendly match with Wavertree were defeated by two goals to one. The result was very unsatisfactory as they had quite as much of the play as their opponents and was due to a temporary laxity in defence.
