The Examiner, Saturday, June 22, 1907.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Reader? of this column are invited to assist ii. making it interesting and helpful to social workars by contributing themselves.

Correspondents must write on one side of the raper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plums may be used, but the real. name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S. must he addressed to John Workman, " Examiner" Offfce, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

Has it ever struck you That the rich and poor have only one way of entrance into the world, and one way of departure ? That the Church has three special possessions and treasures—the Bible, which proclaims man's freedom; Baptism, his equality; the Lord's Supper, his brother- hood?

That all are equal within the Church's gate? That the Gaspel of Jesus Christ was pre-ercinently the gospel of the poor? That the real secret of success in the performance of the clergy's duty towards the working classes lies in their approaching them as fellow men? That the old order changeth and yieldeth place to new?

That a man is only a man as he makes life and nature happier to us That without the rich heart, wealth is an ugly beggar

That we shall pass through the world but once, therefore, any good thing that we may do, or any kindness we may show, let us do it now ? That Socialism is Christianity translated into practical politics ? That the land belongs to the whole of the people? That trespassers will not be prosecuted in Heaven ? That by setting up wealth as the sole stimulus to human endeavour you have bred a ract of thin brained egotists, who regard the community only as their prey ? That Socialists have a higher conception of the duty of the State toward the individual than their adversaries have?

_ That no man has any right to call upon, or to make another do for him, what he will not do for the other?

That every individual of the human race has a full equal right to the earth, and to the services of others in return for his own, and there is no other right inherent in the human race. In other words—all men, by nature, have equal rights ? These questions are well worth your consideration. On your answer to such like questions will depend in a great measure the future welfare of each and all. Think them over and so put your minds in the best trim for answering them. We want to make the world brighter and happier for everybody, and if we do our best we shall succeed. I shall we pleased to hear from any of my readers. Mv attention has been called to a paragraph in last E.Jattirday'a " Eratniner " headed The Painlor i& h. Workhouse. James Clarke, a painter was charged at the Warrington Borough Court with neglecting to maintain himself, whereby he became chargeable to the Union. He pleaded not guilty and the case was dismissed. Mr. Basil Shearer, who prosecuted, in reply to Alderman Tinnion, said that probably defen- dant made 11 a week more than he cost the Guardians but he wished to point out that if men who were able to work were allowed to go into the Workhouse the would require ion Workhouses. That remark is some- what ambiguous. Does he mean to pint out that ; there are sufficient " out of works" in Warrington to ' fill ten Workhouses ? If so it would be interesting to know why they are out of work. Or, does he mean to say that men who are already in work would give 'up their jobs to reside in the Workhouse? That hardly appears to be the reason judging from the fact that the Guardians pr)babl7 made a profit of 11 a week out of Clarke and theV could not have done that unless he had works(' for Nista. Mr. Whitham, whose articles are so much appreci- ated by readers of the " Eraminer," has been good enough to send me some S Icialist newspapers, cuttings, and pamphlets. I take this opportunity of thanking him for them,and shall make use of them as opportunity presents itself. POVERTY.

" Sir " said Johnson, "all the arguments wkich are brought to represent poverty as no evil, show it to be evidently a great evil. You never find people labour- ing to convince you that you may live very happily upon a plentiful fortune." He knew what he was talking of, that rugged old master of commons,mse. Poverty is of course a relative thing ; the term has reference, above all, to one's standiog as an intellectual being. If lam to believe the newspapers, there are title-bearing men and women in England, who, had they an assured income of live and-twenty shillings per week, would have no right to call themselves poor, for their intel- lectual needs are those of a stable-boy or scullery wench. Give me the same income and I can live, but I am poor indeed. You tell me that money cannot buy the things most precious Tour commonplace proves that you have never known the lack of it. 'Then I think of all the sorrow and the barrenness that has been wrought in my life by want of a few more pounds per annum than I was able to earn, I stand aghast at m-mey's significance. What kindly joys have I lost, those simple forms of happiness to which every heart has claim, because of poverty. Meetings with those I loved made impossible year after year ; sadness, misunderstanding, nay, cruel alienation, arising from inability to do the things I wished, and which I might have done had a little money helped me; endless Instances of homely pleasure and contentment curtailed or forbidden by narrow means. I have lost friends merely through the constraints of my position; friends I might have made have remained strangers to me, solitude of the bitter kind, the solitude which is enforced at times when mind or heart longs for companionship, often cursed my life solely because I was poor. I think it would scarce be an exaggeration to say that there is no moral good which has Rot to be paid for in coin of the realm. "Poverty," said Johnson again, "is so great an evil, end pregnant with so much temptation, so munh misery, that I cannot but earnestly enjoin you to avoid it."

HENRY RTECROFT. THE MUNICIPALIZATION OF THE DRINK TRADE.

It is probable that all serious minded people who desire the well being of the nation and the improve- ment of the race are agreed as to the evils of the present methods of drink distribution. We all admit the evil but we differ from each other on the question of remedies ; some short-sighted enthusiasts argue that all beverages of an alcoholic character are evil in their effects and altogether superfluous and that therefore the effective remedy for the evils of the" Drink Tra3e " is to prohibit the trade altogether. It is true that many medical men assert that alcohol is of no service to, and has none but banefu effects on, the human body but other doctors assert quite the contrary and recommend the use of alcohol Whatever may be the truth, and when doctors. differ the layman must decide for himself, there is no overlooking the fact that men who regularly indulge in a moderate amount of beer, wine or whisky appear in most cases to enjoy just as good health and live as long as the total abstainer and whilst this fact is patent to everyone of an observant character it is no use talking of drink and poison as if the two were syn onomous.

I The average man has centuries ago proved to his I own satisfaction that it is possible to purchase with a draught of the red red wine that freedom from care and worry, and that elevation or spirit which tinges the grey sky of fact with the red glow of fancy and makes sad hearts merry. The average man has learned these facts in the school of experience and he has also proved that a moderate indulgence in stimulants is not incompatible with a man remaining healthy, wealthy and wise. It is true that alcohol is superfluous in the sense that we could do without it, incidentally we could also do without houses, clothing and a thousand and one other things which to-day makes up the sum of our lives, but very rightly our old friend the average man has got these comforts and he means to retain them. I therefore think I can safely say that the universal use of alcohol proves that the majority of people desire it and they will have it. In spite of many attempts on the part of prohibitionists the supply of alcohol in any large centre of population has never been stopped, nor do I admit that the man who does not believe in strong drink himself has any right to interfere with other people unless they are acting in an anti-social , fashion and abusing alcohol. To set my conclusions! out clearly I will say: 1 „.

" The majority of men like a stimulant." " The majority of men 'mill have a stintuiant." "Th. maiority of men have a right to oi/flu/ants in noileraliess."

And this latter in my opinion !should be the goal of all

===== 0002601_19070622_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
sensible reformers, i.e., to reduce the evils of strong WANTED A REVOLUTION drink to a minimum by curtailing exce;s. The scheme which will mend the public house, of Social Conditions. eliminate,all its evil features, and remove all its

MR. J. R. CLYNES, 15 P., CRITICISES LIBERAL AND TORY PARTIES.

inducements to over-indulgence, will do more for the cause of true temperance than all the lectures of the Blue Ribbon Army. With the solitary exception of the SOCIALIST SOIUTION Labour and the Government. that is to say the municipalisation of the drink trade A Socialist demonstration was announced to be held no other suggested remedy proposes to do anything at Bridge Foot on Saturday esening, but owing to the for the betterment of the public-house, but rather do wretched weather it was abandoned. Instead a public all they begin by treating the drink trade as an unclean meeting was held at the rooms of the Independent thing, and propose to end it by wiping it out. Labour Party, Friars' Gate Mr. E. Horrocks presiding When will the Temperance folk cease to cry for the over a good attendance. Isfr. J. R. Clynee, M. P. for moon of total or local prohibition, and give sensible North-East Manchester, was the principal speaker. The CHAIRMAN said Mr. Clynes did not come to them men a hand to get rid of some of the as a stranger, for they had had the pleasure of hearing VTILS OF TO-DAT. him on many occasions. They were pleased with his Given sensible legislation and enlightehed public conduct in the House and believed that in him Labour opinion, and it omild be made as difficult to get drunk had a capable representative. as it is now easy. Will our Temperance friends help Mr. CLTNES said Socialists were charged with enter- or hinder the comincr of that sensible legislation? taming a preference for one party and with STANLEY LORD. unduly and unfairly opposing the Liberals in the politics of England. It had been asserted that their plan was to handicap and damage the Liberal party in connection with national questions. Those who talked about the hostility of Labour to the Liberal party overlooked the facts. He was not going to try and prove that they were the friends of the Liberal party any more than that they were the friends of the Tories. They were the opponents of both, in so far as they could see both to be opposed to the true interests of the mass of the people. They in no sense entertained or expected any preferential treat- ment in regard to either or both parties. At the last election there were sixty-five Labour candidates who stood under the banner of different organizations and the answer to the charge that they were merely seeking to damage the position of the Liberals was seen in the fact that only six out of the 30 candidates sought to secure seats then occupied by Liberal representati ves.lf anyone bad ground to complain against the action of the Labour party it was the Tory party, certainly more than the Liberal party. However, they were not very ranch concerned about those smaller differences of political warfare. Socialists had devoted time and Attention to industrial and social subjects, freed from the prejudices and restraints of political attachments,and formed their own opinions. Witlaout offence he claimed to correctly describe the Liberal or the Tory as a man who had merely inherited certain party prejudices and had grown up in a Liberal or Conservative atmosphere. He simply went on believing what his father believed. Alluding to the Jarrow election, Mr. Clynes said it had been stated that because the Irish Nationalists had thought fit to bring out a candidate, the Labour party intended, as an act of retaliation, to contest seats in the constituencies in Ireland now held by Irish Nationalists. He could assure them that an idea so , wild had never entered into their heads. They never ' for a moment thought of anything so foolish and extravagant as an act of retaliation of that kind. They fully recognised the right of the Irish Nationalist party to bring out candidates irrespective of the views or convenience of other parties. He believed the intervention of the Irish Nationalist candidate at Jarrow would improve the chances of Mr. Pete Curran. Continuing Mr. Clynes said that I social reform was often no more than an empty phrase, and had no real deep meaning or significance for men who employed it. To the Socialist or Labour man social reform meant changes in our social con- dition, which would give to the mass of the people a larger share of the wealth made by Labour than the people now received. They could not have that kind of reform without attacking the interests and privileges of those who at present owned and controlled the main ' sources of wealth production. The Tory party had nothing whatever to offer the people now beyond what was termed tariff reform, reduced recently to pre- ferential treatment with the colonies. If there was anything in the way of a public soandel in politics, he thought it was seen in the way the Tories had endeavoured to use Colonial sentiment, and the recent Conference between the Colonial Premiers for mere sordid party purposes. They did their level best to make the Union Jack a kind of party asset and had striven all along in connection with that agitation to make use of national and Colonial senti- ment for mere Conreryative purposes. They said a 10 per oent duty on manufactured articles would give us an inoome of 13 millions a year. The effect of the proposal, however, was to keep foreign goods out, so that. they could not have the 13 millions duty and the benefit of the transaction as well. What was 13 millions a year? It was a lot less than we paid in the way of rent every month to the landlord classes. They were the people who had the preference. Socialists could not see any gain industrially or commercially from this tinkering with tariffs. They looked upon the Tory party as the traditional enemies of the interests and the just claims of the working classes. It was a party supported by a number of wealthy and privileged persons, many of whom had land through the mere law of inheritance or who had large establishments in which thousands of workingmen were toiling and mak- ine wealth for them • and by many whose power in making the law had been inherited as well. The Liberal party, despite its powerful place in the legislature to-day, was largely a helpless body so far as real and wholesome changes for the welfare of the country were concerned. Any Radical who happened to be present would, he supposed, admit that he had hoped to live to see the day when his party would have a great majority in the House of Commons—a majority perhaps greater than any preceding party had ever had—when there would be a Liberal Chancellor of the Exchequer ; when there would be freedom from the House of Lords in regard to the social changes that were desired in the interests of the people. All these things the Liberal party at this moment possessed ; and what had they seen? They had seen a budget introduced and so far agreed to and supported by the mass of the Liberals in the country, which left the social and industrial condi- ditions as they were before. The only change effected was a little relief for men who paid income tax. No working man benefited by any proposal so far made. The Budget was free from the House of Lords.and he wished the working classes only understood how deeply they could effect social reform if they had a courageous and con- vinced Chancellor of the Exchequer, without going near the House of Lords. (Applause). What did the Government promise for next session? There was no mention of any great social change. Truly, we were promised Licensing legislation which would interfere and he dare say rightly, with the present privileges of the brewers. They were promised an Education Bill which once more would put into the melting pot all the seething differences of mind, faith and conscience that set men against,settling any serious social questions which werepressine for attention. They could not look with any great degree of hope to the Liberal party despite its enormous majority. Their relation to the Liberal party was a relation of friendly co-operation in so far as they were going in the directioreof Labour's good, but it was also a relation of open hostility and strong criticism vvhenever they felt that attitude needful in the interests of the workers. Alluding to the resolu- tion to be moved by the Premier with regard to the Home of Lords, Mr. Clyne' said his conviction was that its terms fell far short of what even the ordinary Liberal workingmen was hoping for. Mr. Clyne* dealt with the constitution of the House of Lords and main- tained that many of the members kad shown themselves unfitted to take an honoured or usefel part in public business. What were these brave words about the House of Lords coming to in regard to Liberal deeds? The resolution simply expressed the conviction that a law if passed successfully in one Parliament in the House of Commons must become law, regardless of the action of the House of Lords. That was quite insufficient. Tt was no real battle cry to fight the lords. They found the same hesitancy in other pro- posals for legislation. The Liberals were brave on the platform, hut when it came to framing things for the House of Commons their courage seemed to have filtered through, and they did not propose in Bills the same stiff terms which they proclaimed from platforms. Mr. Clynee proceeded to argue in favour of land reform, and wild the Liberals ought to transform platform fervour into the terms of real proposals. The struggle now was for better social and economic conditions, and the other parties largely left those questions alone. Society could not be left at the mercy of powerful and privi- leged persons, and it was the duty of statesmen and of Parliament to regulate and order society for the common good of the human family. (Applause.)

THE GOSPEL OF HOPE

Sir,—l have been deeply interested in your" Voice of Labour "column, and, like your Jarrow-on-Tyne reader, I believe you are "doing a good work." Your able, still courteous advocacy will, I venture to predict, prove an effective source of propaganda by directing public attention to the humanising influence 'of the great gospel of Socialism, and further, by a consistent erample of broad-minded toleration you will, I hope, be the means of eventually raising to a higher plane the general tone of political controversy in Warrington, which is, alas, at present, far too personal, spiteful, narrow-minded and party-pre- judiced. The Press of this country often erroneously describe us (the Socialists) as mere pessimists and superficial observers ; but, as a matter of fact, we preach a gospel of hope not a creed of despair ; 'whilst, as a result of a careful systematic study of scientific sociology, we are firmly convinced that "we have a cause that cannot fail." The evolution of our industrial and economic condi- tions, coupled with the great social evils arising out those conditions, points to the conclusion that the civilized nations must either live and work together for the common good, or else they will be eventually destroyed in revolution and ruin. 1 staunch faith in the essential goodness of human nature and a determination that our crying social evils shall not continue, have never been so strong and vigorous as they are to day. Education and improved transit are gradually breaking down national barriers, race-hatreds and religious prejudices. The men and women workers of the world are learning from one another the reasons why the many are sad, destitute and poverty•stricken, whilst the few revel in luxury and comparative idleness. Now is the time for all earnest social reformers to puta side their petty, sectional, selfish interests so that by an intelligent, united, incessant campaign we may more efficiently propagate the principles of our great international Socialist movement, and hold up our _noble ideals to the whole human race.

If we all stand loyal and remain true to one another in this great fight, then, although we shall not live to see the complete realization of our hopes and aspira- tions, we shall be enabled to hand on the certainty of success to others. Then, when those who come after look back on our life's work, they will excl.itn with reverential awe and admiration, (in the words of H. I. Hyndman). "This was glory —this true domination, these men buibled on eternal foundations their might, majesty dominion and power." Yours fraternally,

JUSTICE.

"THAT TIRED FEELING."

Important Advice to the Ailing.

Enervating weather upsets the system and gives rise to " that tired feeling." The hardworking housewife, although she may feel weary and worn-out cannot neglect her ditties in the home. The toiling husband has also to try and work off his sick headache,bilious attack, or general debility. At this season many of us ~re liable to suffer from sickness, lassitude, and depression, which very often, unless taken in hand at once are forerunners of serious illness. A natural tonic-laxative for the stomach and liver is the surest and safest remedy ; and there is no comparison between Chas. Forde's Bile Beans and ordinary pills sold at a lower price. Your health is a serious matter; and only the genuine reputable medicine can be relied upon to produce in your case the best and most permanent improvement a result far outweighing the possibly slightly increased cost per box. Chas. Fordes Bile Beans are a family medicine of proved superiority for young and old. No household should ever be without, it handy one-and- three-halfpenny or two-and-nine box.

you read the life of Napoleon, boys, ant grow enthusiastic over his wonderful heroieni and military genius, and wish in your very hearts that you might have been with the 0051- queror as he scaled the Alps and besieged great cities, and overcame every obstacle in his did you ever query what it was that gave him such wonderful control over his soldiers, so that they would dare anything for their leader? Did you suppose that he was admired by them as a sort of god, and, living in an almosphere far above the French soldiery, ruled them as Jupiter did the world in ancient times, hurling his thunderbolts among them if they dared dis- obey his command? Did you think that be- cause for years he was acknowledged the firreateit man in Europe, if not in the world, that he controlled men and nations through fear? If you did, you are greatly mistaken. Napoleon was Napoleon when but a boy. He had when very young the stuff in him that 'pekes men, makes heroes, and the discipline and hard work to which he subjected himself When a boy was one of the greatest reasons of his later success, for it helped him to enter into sympathy with his soldiers, and so control them. He was S. general who said., "Come, follow me," instead of, "Go, and I'll follow," to those under his command.

I want to relate to you an incident in his history which occurred when he was but fifteen years of age, and was a prophecy of future greatness. He was sent at that time to a mili- tary school at Paris, which was luxuriously fur- nished for the children of the aristocracy. It had been founded for them, and as they had been accustomed to every indulgence at their homes, here they were to be as carefully looked after. A writer tells us that "each of the three hundred young men assembled in this school had a servant to groom his horse, to brush his boots, and to perform all other neces- sary menial services. The cadet reposed on a luxurious bed, and was fed with sumptuous vian.ds. There were few lads of fifteen who would not have been delighted with the dignity, the ease, the independence of that style of living. Napoleon, however, immediately saw that this was by no means the training requisite to prepare officers for the toils and hardships of war. Although so young, he addressed an energetic memorial to the governor, urging the banishment of this effeminacy and voluptuous- ness from the military school. He argued that the students should learn to groom their own horses, to clean their own armour, and to per- form all those services, and to inure themselves to those privations which would prepare them for the exposure and toils of actual service." The historian adds, that no incident in the childhood or in the life of Napoleon shows more decisively than this his energetic, self- reliant, commanding character. The wisdom, the bravery, and the foresight which usually come with mature years were exhibited here. And the military school which many years after Napoleon established at Fontainebleau, and which obtained such world-wide celebrity. was founded upon the model of this youthful memorial. There is no doubt that one dis- tinguishing cause of the extraordinary popu- larity which Napoleon afterwards secured, was to be found in the fact that through life he called upon no one to encounter perils or en- dure hardships which he was not perfectly ready himself to encounter and to endure. It would be well if our boys would remember this incident in the life of one who is doubt- less a hero to them' and sltheugli they may not aspire to become Napoleons, and we should hope would not be successful if they did, understand that to acquire any commanding position in life, to be successful in any depart- ment of work in the world's great industries, the heroic and military spirit of Napoleon is necessary. You must have high aspirations, real courage, a determination to understand practically even the menial departments of work, and a willingness to. labour energetically yourselves, if you ever expect to get toward the top of the ladder, and control other men and achieve success in life. You will find it true that nearly, if not all, our great merchants cc- gan their career working hard for small wages, but doing what was given them to do with a will, and a heartiness and a thoroughness which merited as it received the confidence of their employers, and brought them 3ucc2r,sive pro- motions.

OLD AGE PENSIONS.

Sir,—Seeing that so much has recently been said and written on the above question, perhaps if I state my opinion on this scheme I might raise one or two points on which may follow a little discussion, as there are, without doubt, certain phases of the question that seem to me well worth considering, and may not be so easily dealt with as as many of us are apt to believe.

; Let us first take the case in favour of Old Age ; Pensions. Personally, lam in favour of the measure, as I feel sure if worked on truly democratic lines, its advantages will outweigh its disadvantages. Considering that it costs this country twenty million pounds every year for the up-keep of pauperism, with its degrading method of dealing with our less fortunate human beings, whose greatest crime is poverty, I think that to grant old age pensions to all persons over 65 years of age a sum of ten shillings per week, would indeed be cheap, and would rid the country of the present poor law system, that is a disgrace to is as i Englishmen. That s one reason why I favour old age pensions. Another reason is that it would lift a great burden from the minds of- our industrious poor, seeing that they would have something better to look forward to in their declining years than the Work- house. It would have a wonderful effect upon the home life of these people. There are thousands, nay, I may say millions of this class who go short of many home comforts in the shape of good food, good clothing, and the hundred and one little things that go to make a home bright and cheerful in order that they may put a little away for a rainy day, or when the time comes that they cannot through old age labour any more. This, to my mind, Mr. Editor, is the most important part of the whole question and if our legislators would only step down from their high pedestals of officialism and materialism into the arena of humanism and look at the question from the point of view of the people's home life, then I feel sure that Old-age Pensions for the aged would soon be realised and many British hearts and homes made brighter. The Government that would grant a pension on the scale I have mentioned, would win a lasting support from the working classes and the prssent Government with its large majority never had a better opportunity. The people are tired of promises. For though this question of Old-age Pensions has been dangled before the eyes of the people for years, it has never material- ised, but simply been advertised with a view to catch- ing votes from the workers during elections times. Another point in favour of this measure is, seeing that the people would be relieved of the dread of pauperism in their old-age, they would spend more • freely and so create a better sale in the home markets and thus in turn would find work for many who are now unemployed, without ever aolving the unemployed problem. With your permission, Sir, there are a few points I would like to mention here that are well worth con- sidering. Firstly, seeing that there is an increasing number of employers of labour who now apply the "too old at forty" rule to men in their employ, what are we going to do with these men so discharged, during the intervening years? They are branded as being too old to work, yet they would be some twenty years too young to receive the state pension. Secondly, the granting of Old Age Pensions would, as I have above stated, lead to a a brisker home trade as people would spend more freely, and in my opinion the retailers of food, clothing and other commodities, would raise their prices as a consequence of the greater demand for these necessaries of life, and in turn the land and property owners may follow by raising rents, and this would have the partial effect of taking from the people that which was intended for their benefit. At any rate it would take a lot of the 'polish ' off the Old Age Pensions for the poor worker and would put a little-more polish' on rents and profits of shopkeepers and land and property owners. I may mention in passing, that I feel convinced that if a national minimum wage were granted the same would be the result and the people would be little if any better off! Thirdly, if old age pensions were granted at once we would still have our army of unemployed, and I feel sure that if we could solve this problem it would prove a greater blessing to the workers even than old age pensions, for then the workers could demand a wage well above the existence stage and provide their own pension fund for the aged. Fourthly—and this is a ticklesome ' side of the question—what are we goiag to do with the "wont works," the thriftless and criminal class? Of course, Mr. Editor, I, as a Socialist, am well aware of the fact that many of these people owe their position and condition largely through their surroundings and to circumstances over which they have had no control, but still, that does not alter the fact that they are here and will want dealing with. Are they to be placed on the same level as the indus- trious workers or are they to be debarred from par- ticipating in this pension scheme? It is, as I have said, a " ticklesome " question to deal with. It will be seen tha4 although I favour old age pensions as being a step forward, still I have tried to point out in the latter portion of this letter there are difficulties to be met, and after all it simply amounts to a palliative and will fall a long way short of solving the problem of poverty. The only way that leads to the emancipation of the workers lies in the application of Socialism. May we soon arrive.—Yoars ecc., TRADE UNIONIST. ,

AY D:

la. J wed to say 'that lam st. oy deep, blue sea, and to tell you the truth I begin to feel quite young again. I told you last week I would let you know if I paddled—rather— it seemed quite like old times, or it would have done if it hadn't been for the old crabs, shinkly stones, andejellyfish. Where I am staying there are lots of dear boys and girls paddling and digging, and I often wonder whether any of them are relations of mine, because as I have never seen any of you it stands to reason I should not know if I happened to meet you. I wish it were possible to know every one of you, but I can't think how it could be done; at any rate I shall always be glad to receive photographs of any member of the Corner who cares to send one. I have not received a great number of donkeys yet, SO I must hold over what I have until some more arrive; under these conditions I must award the prizes next week, when I hope to again be in the midst of grinding toil. Until then, my dears, adieu. -UNCLE NED.

--.--- JOHNNY PIG. BY MARGARET EYTINGE.

Little Johnny Eataway's playmates called him "Johnny Pig"; and I don't wonder that they did, for he was •one of the greediest boys that ever lived.

Almost every day when dinner was over, and he had eaten so much he could not eat any more, he would beg his mamma, with. a dread- ful whine, not to give what was left of the pudding or pie—which wasn't much, I can assure you—to anyone else, but to put it away in the cupboard so that he might "eat it by-and- by."

And often he would stand for an houx at a time before the windows of a. bakery or candy store, with the tears running down his cheeks, in the deepest grief because he could not eat everything he saw there. And he would hallow men who were selling fruit from street to street, just as other boys follow the soldiers, or a monkey on a hand- organ, in hopes that, at last, to get rid of him, they would give him an apple, or an orange, or a banana.

Well, late one very cloudy afternoon, Johnny Pig was coming from the druggist's with a. small bottle of paregoric for the bafby, who had a pain (paregoric was the only thing that oould be swallowed that he could be trusted with), when he saw a man in front of him carrying a basket half full of pretty pink paper packages. Johnny got as near as he could to this man, and sniffed at the basket.

It smelled delicious! Just like ma.mxna's kitchen on• cake-baking days. The man ran up every step, and rang every door-bell, and gave one of the packages to who- ever came to the door. - _ .

At lest Johnny Pig, who was by this time a mile from home, and it was fast getting dark, asked the man what they were. "Cakes," said the man. "Gimme one?" begged Johnny. "No," said the man, "I don't give them to little boys." But Johnny kept following and teasing and teasing until the man—it was quite dark now— said, "Well, as I have only a few left and I want to go to my supper you may have one." Johnny snatched it without even a "thank you" kgreedy boys are never polite), sat down on -the nearest doorstep, laid the bottle of paregoric by his side, tore off the pretty pink paper, and took a bite—a big bite. - _

And then he jumped up, knocking over the bottle and breaking it into flindens, and stamped, and choked and spluttered, and wiped his mouth again and again on the sleeve of his new jacket. It was a sake of soap/

HOW TOM DRESSES. AT HOME. A grimy face, a muddy boot, A broken lace, a shabby suit; With threadbare knee and dusty coat, And dirty collar round his throat. OUT VISITING. Now see his face is all aglow, He's tied both laces in a bow; He's combed his hair, he's brushed hie suit, There's not a speck on either boot; His collar now is new and clean— A neater boy I've never seen. Yet Tom should be, beyond a doubt, As clean at home as when he's out. For those who dress 'mid friends to roam, Should dress as well for those at home. DAISY STOCKS.

A SHORT CONVERSATION,

The celebrated physician, Dr. Abernethy, was famous for the brevity and bluntness of his answers; he never used a word more than was necessary. One day a lady, who knew his peculiarity, came to him and held out her finger, without a word. "Cut ?" asked the doc- tor. "Bite," answered the patient. " Dog I" "Parrot." "Go home and poultice," said Abernethy. The next day the finger was again shown. " Better ?" was the doctor's question. 'Worse." "Poultice again." Lastly, when the finger was at length cured, the doctor even went so far as to compliment his patient. " Better?" he asked. "Quite well." "Good. You are the most sensible woman I ever met. Good day." ELIZABETH STOCKS.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

St. Barnabas' Church. The Education Question.

The patronal festival in connection with this church was continued on Sunday when there were good con- gregations. The Rev. Canon 'Morley Stevenson preached at the choral celebration of the Holy Communion in the morning his text being: "That our sons may grow up as the young plants, and that our daughters may be as the polished corners of the temple." The ideal system of education, he said,sought to bring strength to the body, usefulness to the mind,and the beauty of holiness to the soul. Ho urged the importance of teaching children the Christian faith and said that in order to do this the Church of England had built thousands of schools in this country--schools which had been built out of the contribi.tions and offerings of members of that Church in order that that kind of education might be given to children. Last year a Bill was brought into Parliament which would have dealt a very severe and, he believed, a very unjust blow at Church schools—a Bill which made it no longer necessary or obligatory that religious teaching should be given in tke schools, which might have put an end to religious teaching of any kind and certainly would have put an end to the teaching of tkeir own Church. If the Bill had been passed, they would have had removed from them at one stroke the schools which they and their fathers had built, and they would have been used as schools in which the most important part of education would have been lost. He thought they might say that in the providence of God that Bill no longer existed. But they must be prepared for this—that there might be again and very probably would be a great struggle on the part of those who wished to see the Church schools taken from them. Therefore Church people must be prepared, without bitterness or uncharitableness, but calmly and quietly sul'a duty to defend their schools to the best of their power. In the meantime the best defence they could have was to make their schools so good and to see that they carried out their work so efficiently that everyone would say it would be a shame to atop the good work which was being done in them. In the afternoon a children's service was held and the Vicar performed the cormony of blessing the hand- some new Sunday school banner, which bears the figure of SOBarnabas. There was a large congregation at Evensong, when the Rev. Canon Kempthorne, rector of Liverpool, was the preacher. He also spoke on the education question, his text being " Without me, ye can do nothing." He said that as Churchpeople their idea of education was that the intellect should get the best possible training, but they wanted to have the training of character and that mist be based on the Christian faith as proved from the Bible. In their Bible teaching they wanted to have clear' plain,strong answers to different questions. They wanted the children to know Whose they were and Whom they served. They wanted them to know what as Christians they ought to believe and do, and above all, where they could find the motive and the power to do it. If undenoininational teaching meant,as its beat authori- ties said it meant, non-credal, ethical, literary teaching of the Bible, it was absolutely helpless and impotent to give the moral training they desired. Churchmen did not ask for special privileges for themselvea,but desired equality and justice all round. They believed the only right principle was that the parent should be allowed to say what sort of religious teaching he desired his children should reeeive, and that no other should be given. Teachers also should have absolute liberty to give or not to give the particalar religious teaching of the school in which they were placed. The choir sang the anthem "I will pray the Father " (Simper), Mr. T. Evans presiding at the organ. The collections were on behalf of the day and Sunday school funds.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Hill Cliffe Baptist Church.

There were largo congregations at Hill Cliffe Church on Sunday on the occasion of the celebration of the anniversary. Sermons wert preached afternoon and evening by the Rev. C. W. Skemp, of Eceles. A children's flower service was held in the morning, when Mr. James tienworthy preeided and delivered an intereating address. Mr. H. Law, of Stockton Heath, gave some valuable advice to the scholars. A number of specially selected hymns were sung in a praiseworthy manner. During the day special anthems were snag by the choir. Mr. A. 13. Fieawortby presided at the organ at the morning service, and Mr. Peter Harrison at the afternoon and evening services. The collections for the day amoanted to £33 43.—The scholars annual picnic took place on Monday, when the weather, fortuastely, was line, and a most enjoyable day was spent at Mr. Bradford's, Retrdish Hall, Grappenball, to which place the children were conveyed in buses' and harks. Gaines were played and refreshments served.

REMARKABLE OCEAN MIRAGE.

A remarkably distinct mirage was seen by the passengers on board the liner Philadelphia when the French liner La Lorraine appeared upside down in the sky ahead. An exchange of wireless messages showed that the La Lorraine wus far beyond the horizon. Me vision continued for an hour and a half.

CUT THIS OUT.

The vagaries of the English Climate, the stress of business. and the hundred and one little •rorries that one is daily subjected to must sooner or later affect one's health, and there comes a time when it is not possible to ignore the fact that one is run down and needs recnnerating. Yet most people have a natural disinclination to go to a doctor on these occasions and one goes on with "the, daily round " in a hopeless fashion, trying to persoade oneself that it will be all right in a day or two. But it is not safe to leave the remedy to time alone, and the following was prescrbed by an eminent authority to meet such a case : Tincture of Buchu, one ounce ; Karok Compound, one ounce : Syrup of Lemon, two ounces. Any chemist will dis- pense this, or it may be made up at home by shaking well in a bottle. Take one teaspoonful after each meal and at bed-time. It is perfccty harmless and simply assists Nature to re-assert itself. In Kidney and Urinary Troubles it has wonderful curative powers. This prescription should be cut out, and kept for ' reference, as even if you do not need it at present,tbere are times when it will be highly beneficial to you.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

"GETTING INTO STRIDE."

Another letter from Mr. A. H, Crosfleld, M.P.

Sir,—Next week you will be chiefly interested in the question of the House Of Lords, and it will then be difficult to find time and space for discussing our ordin- ary legislative record and programme, with which in my previous letter I promised to deal. May I then refer to it this week ; and in the first place draw attention to the work of Mr. Lloyd George at the Board of Trade. Let us compare the work of the present Government in developing commerce, with that of its predecessor. I need not remind you that the Sugar Convention is to be either denounced, or so altered as to abolish the prohibition upon sugar grown in Russia, the Argentine, and other countries which retain bounties. Immense industries—a very large pro_ portion of those that cater for the people's supply of food and drink—depend upon sugar as raw material,and will therefore feel the advantages of a free market in which to buy. The Board of Trade is moreover co-operating with the Foreign Office in order to effect a complete re-organ- isation of the Consular Service, which has been far too retch l'E T the control of aristocratic families, who have often a congenital inclination to despise the mtindane, (yet vitally important!) processes of buying and selling. We have before us at the present time a Bill which settles the question of Owners' Risks upon railways. This is a Private Member's measure, but it is significant that obstruction to it has come from Tories like Sir Frederick Banbury who, on the public platform, make an especial point of the necessity for "defending our commerce." It would perhaps be a little unkind to ask why there should be this strange discrepancy between their teaching outside the House, and their practice within it ! The most important of these Trade Reforms is how- ever the Patents Bill, which has been carried through Committee in the teeth of the varied influences exerted by the American Trusts. When this Bill becomes law, as it is morally certain to do this year, every holder of a patent in this country will have to work his patent here ; otherwise it becomes void. The old plan of excluding British industry by taking out patents which there was no intention of working in this country thus becomes impossible. In more ways than one we in Warrington, with our great leather industry, have an interest in the fact that the clauses dealing with restrictive agreeements as to machinery will save that industry from the threat of gradual strangulation by a powerful American Trust. Here is good positive work. Liberals are entitled to ask why in all their years of office the Tories so seldom attempted useful commercial legislation of this kind. But I have yet to mention the Government's still larger measures, as for instance the three Land Bills,for England, Scotland and Ireland respectively. In Ireland of course, land legislation has been proceeding for years, and the Evicted Tenants Bill really helps to complete the task of a generation. The Scottish Bill has also grown on': of the past, for it is an extension and development of the Crofters' Act which was passed by the last Liberal Government. We all know how the peasantry of Scotland have left the land for one reason or other, the claims of productive agricultural industry having in too many cases been sacrificed to other con- siderations. I have never run down sport, and am not going to do so now. But Dr. Johnson once told us that " moderation " is the finest word in the English language, and though some of us may, and I for one do, value sport and its influence on English character, its claims may be pushed too far. They have been pushed too far in Scotland. Too many people have for that or for other reasons left their native soil. Everyone deplores—deeply deplores—that fact and all its conse- quences. And yet the Government's proposals to arrest this process have met with deadly opposition from the Tories. In Standing Committee several whole days of obstruction passed before one line of the Bill was carried, and Mr. Balfour openly threatened to use the power—the too unrestricted aiul irresponsible power—of his friends in another place for the purpose of over-riding the decision and nullifying the eitorts of those who sent us to Parlia- ment.

Mr. Harcourt's English Land Bill was so good and strong a Bill and —experts tell me—so cleverly drafted that the Tories dare not oppose its second reading; but here again we have to watch our opponents closely, and I was glad to hear Mr. Harcourt announce in advance thas he will not agree to weakening amendments. After all the object of these reforms is far from being a dis- play of unfriendliness towards the landed interests. We cannot allow the British race to deteriorate as it will deteriorate if we lose our country-bred folk. And so we must secure to the agricultural labourer free access to the soil, and the opportunity of cultivating a small holding where he has the skill and energy to accomplish this task. These Land Bills are great measures, and having yesterday attended the deputation which was received by the Prime Minister, loan further assure you that the whole question of Land Valuation is under final inrestigation by the Gorernment with a viewto legislation next year.

I cannot say what will be the action of the House of Lords towards our programme, but there can be no denying that so far as we in the Commons have the opportunity under the Constitution of carrying out our pledges, we are loyally seeking to justify your confi- dence.

I might mention one or two other little Bills. Medical Inspection of children in our Public Elemen- tary Schools will, I hope and believe,be authorised this year. It sounds a small measure, but it affects millions of the young and it will of course go far to correct the terrible evils described in the Report on Physical Detonation—which the Tories first published and then ignored. Another little Bill securing immediate notifi- cation of births will help suitable agencies to see to the proper feeding of young children, upon which infant life so largely depends. Finally, may I remind you that we are making a great effort to secure the passa7e of our old friend the " Deceased Wife's Sister Bill." Prejudice (not to mention fanaticism) proverbially dies hard, and this question has been no exception to the rule. Our opponents are fighting hard, and resorting to all hinds of strategy. But their numbers are getting gradually small and b3autifully less, and their fate is sealed. Four hundred of us have signed the memorial which has been this week presented, pressing the Government to take charge of this measure. I will not add more than the suggestion that if you take this letter and add it on to my last letter, and then remember that I have still to deal in a further letter with the House of Lords, you will comae to the con- clusion that so far from slacking off in our second Session, we are really getting into our stride.—Yours etc,

A. IL CROSFIELD.

London, June 20, 1907.

===== 0002601_19070622_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Parker and the S.D F.

Reply from Mr. H. Que!ch.

Sir,— A correspondent has sent me a copy of your paper containing a reference by a Mr. Parker to a speech of mine at Plymouth, in which he charges .me with "shuffling," in replying to a question which challenged my statement that the Liberal Government had done nothing." As I pointed out in my reply, the statement obviously had reference to the matter under considera- tion, as no Government could be in office for eighteen months without doing something. if it was only to draw their salaries. As a matter of fact, I said,, the present Clovernment had done a good deal, if we took into account their continuing the policy of their predecessors in foreign affairs and in the Colonies, with the hanging of Kaffirs in Natal, and the execution of Egyptians at Denshawai. But in regard to the questions in which their followers were chiefly interested and expected so much, they had done nothing. As a consequence these followers were disappointed. We Social-Democrats alone were not disappointed, because we had expected nothing, and that was precisely what we got. We had told the people—so readily gulled by politicians of both capitalist parties—that there was nothing more to hope for from the Liberals than from the Tories, and the event had justified our warning. We were therefore in the position of being able to say "we told you so."

To this view Mr. Parker appears to take exception. Well, it would be interesting to know which section of the followers and supporters of the present Government are satisfied with their performance. Are they the Nonconformists, than whom no body of people did more to secure the great Liberal victory? If Mr. Parker thinks so, I would advise him to read some of the speeches and writings of the leaders of Nonconformity, and notably a recent article in the "British Weekly" entitled "Losing the Legions." Are we to look for these satisfied supporters of the Government among those who expected them to sweep away the infamy of Chinese slavery in South Africa immediately on taking office? Or among those who looked for the fulfilment of their promise to amend the Unemployed Workmen Act. Or those who are being thrown out of the National Arsenal at Woolwich to swell the ranks of the unemployed, without any provision being made for them by this sympathetic Liberal Government? Or perhaps we should look for them among the Irish Nationalists, who did so much to put the Liberals into office, but have now rejected their sham Devolution Bill with contempt, and are showing their satisfaction at the performance of the Government by running a candidate against the Liberal at Jarrow? Or shall we find them among our Indian fellow subjects, upon whom "Honest John " Morley is now practising some of the worst methods of the Russian despotism, or of the ancien regime in France, by arresting and imprisoning men without trial, for no other offence than political agitation? If Mr. Parker is satisfied with the perform- ance of the Government, I can assure him that this satisfaction is not generally shared by the supporters of the Government, either inside or outside the House of Commons,and that it is only the Social Democrats who have no occasion for disappointment. Mr. Parker speaks of " a Government that in its first session had passed more measures directly benefiting the workers than any Government of which we had any records." That might easily be true without leaving the Liberals much to boast about, Capitalist Govern- ments are chiefly concerned - with serving capitalist interests, and lam not called upon to show that this Government is any worse than others. My point is that it is no better, and that it obtained office by false pre- tences, by gulling the workers into believing that it was going to do a lot of wonderful things ; things which we Social-Democrats pointed out no capitalist Government could or would do, and that therefore, we are not in the least disappointed, while those who have put their trust in the Liberal Party should have learnt from their experience of the past eighteen months not to repeat the folly. As to the much vaunted achieve- ments on behalf of the working-class, they are two : the Trades Disputes Act and the Workmen's Compensa- tion Amendment Act. In reference to the first, the vital clause, the only thing of any value in the measure, was carried through in spite of the opposition of the Law Officers of the Government, in response to organ- ised Labour pressure, and in the hope that the House of Lords would amend that clause out of the Bill. In regard to the second, an amendment to the Compensation Act was already ovordue, it would have been made whichever party was in power, and it must not be forgotten that it is not to the Liberals, but their opponents, that we owe the introduction into our legislation of this principle of universal compensation for injury. Mr. Parker " protests "against my " assumption that the Socialist party had a right to speak for the Whole of the working classes." This is only because he has not taken the trouble to understand the difference between the Socialist party and all other parties. As against the Socialist party all other parties are con- servative. The Socialist party alone stands for the emancipation of the working-class, the abolition of the last form of human slavery—wage-slavery--through socialisation of the means of production. All other parties assume the perpetuation of the present system of society. based upon the subjection of the working- class to the domination of property. The very most that the best among these other parties aim to do, is to make the conditions of the workers somewhat more tolerable than they are to-day. The Socialist party aims at transforming them entirely. This is why, although there are many among the working-class who still vote for the old parties which would perpetuate their slavery—just as there were many among the South American slaves who fought on the side of their masters—we are perfectly justified in claiming for the Socialist party that it speaks for the whole working class.—Yours faithfully,

H. QI7ELCH, (Editor of" Justice.")

June 15th, 1907.

The Examinee, Saturday June 22, 1907. THE INFIRMARY EXTENSION.

and is an ideal worth working for. Again may I point out within its membership we have:Socialists, Conserva- tives, Liberals, Unionists, and Home Rulers, Church, Free Church and some Catholic members (we admit male and female on equal terms) all of thers holding their own private opinions, and identified with their resp2ctive parties so far as they wish, but united upon this one question of opposition to the use and influence of alcoholic liquor and the trade. Now to municipalize the trade is to make me as a member of the community, practically a shareholder or a part owner in the concern, and,holding the views I do, I don't want to participate in the responsibility. Surely that is consistent. I don't want to take part in the building up, I prefer the pulling down—l do not I quarrel with the phrase that "Socialists and sensible men are synonymous." 1 don't know them all, but some of my earliest and many of my present friends are , Sccialists, and knowing what I do of them would rather agree, but I have known very sensible Conservatives, Liberals, &c. I hardly think it can be claimed as the sole quality of any party, and identification even with a sensible party, does not always convey the quality to the individual. Whether the public houses of Warring- ton are squalid dens or not is a question for the police (who every year certify to their fitness for the purpose) and the Watch Committee From their outward appearance, anyhow we usually term them gin palaces. They appear to be very hand- some and commodious, and I have every reason to believe there is no difficulty in getting temperance drinks, which are given you with as much civility and politeness as though you were buying the most potent of their liquids, in fact some of them have quite a reputation for the supply of dinners &c. No we are not quarrelling with the private lives or the personality of the men or women who sell it. They are the results of the demand for and the law which permits the sale. Their own opinions, with which although we may differ, they have a perfect right to ; their early training, environment and association. We sometimes oppose them for public position,not because of their personality but because we believe they will use that position either in the promotion, or defence of their trade inter- i ests. That is only human nature. We don't quite understand a man being" disinterested" in what he is engaged in, also everyone of them year by year are testified to by the police authorities as being persons of good character. A testmony possessed by no other body of tradesmen in the town. And if under such favourable' conditions, the results are of such a character as to cause Mr. Stanley Lord to deplore them, some of us , may be excused if we are not prepared to take the business from such exceptionally able persons and place it in less experienced hands.We believe the quality of the liquor and its results are no better or no worse whether dispensed by Publican or Parson. Mr. Stanley Lord says " The only sensible remedy (for the evils he enumerates) is the socialist one of municipalization." Being a socialistic remedy does not make it more or less acceptable than it was when the Right Hon. J. Chamberlain, M.P., advocated it years before he entered, Parliament, and it has been under discussion and con- sideration ever since. Who told Mr. Stanley Lord, I wonder, "that the public house is the working man's club ?'; As a workingman all my life I have equally as math right to deny that statement and assert that for the ,one who makes it a rendezvous, there are scores of men amongst the drinkers who do not. I wonder who told Mr. Stanley Lord that total abstainers assumed to themselves all the virtues and attributed all the vices te , those who were not. If all his other statements are equally inaccurate I am afraid we shall have lost all respect for his opinions before he has well I started expanding his ideas. It is true we compare Philip drunk with Philip sober, and assume that the sober man has a keener sense of right and wrong, may control his passions and desires better than when he is under the influence of drink, and the more he is under the influence of drink, the less guarded and more incapable he becomes. This is one of the evils Mr. Stanley Lord himself deplores. But to assume that because a person becomes a total abstainer he is for that reason alone free from vice, is about equally as absurd as to assume because he calls himself a Socialist he is favoured with a superabundance of intelligence. I am afraid I am trespassing too much upon your space, but may I venture to suggest in conclusion to Mr. Stanley Lord, that to call people" fanatics is neither original nor argument, neither are the opening phrases Jf his letter a very good example of philosophic temper- ance of language. Why does he invite people to enter a fray ! rhe consideration of this question should not be a call to battle. Possibly he is young and enthusiastic and has just discovered this question, and is spoiling for a fight.—Believe me, yours very truly, PATRICK O'BRIEN, 125, Wilderspool-road, Jane 18th.

THE REV. H. WALL'S MARRIAGE.

Presentations at Cockhedgp Mission.

The Rev. H. Wall, the minister in charge of Wycliffe Cockhedge Mission, was on the 6th inst. married to Miss Fannie Smith, of Loddon, Norfolk. The event natur- ally aroused great interest among the Cockhedge congre- gation, and at a social gathering on Thursday evening Mr. and Mrs. Wall were cordially welcomed on returning from their honeymoon. Opportunity was also taken to present them with several handsome wedding gifts. There was a large attendance, Mr. Holland presiding. The CHAIRMAN said the friends of Wycliffe Church and Dockhedge Mission would, he was sure, join with him in extending a hearty welcome to Mr. and Mrs. Wall and wishing them every joy and happiness. They hoped, too, that their lives would be made useful in spreading the Gospel in that district and that they might see the salvation of souls. Mr. Wall had brought a lady amongst them who,they felt sure,would share the work there and her presence in many homes would no doubt prove a blessing. He hoped all the Mission helpers would work harmoniously together and that the future might be full of hope for them all. (Applause). Mr. W. H. WALLINGTON made some humorous observations, and also joined in offering to Mr. and Mrs. Wall sincere congratulations. He had been deputed on behalf of the committee and congregation to ask their acceptance of a reminder of that happy occasion in the shape of a hall stand. The gift came from the whole body of the people at the Mission; no section had been excluded. It was a pleasure to them to offer to their pastor and his wife a gift which showed their appreciation of Mr. Wall's services and was an evidence of the hope that their married life would be a great success in every way. They also hoped that the future might have in store even richer blessings for the Mission than had boon experienced in times gone by. (Applause.) Miss SENIOR, on behalf of the girls' class, then pre- sented Mr. and Mrs. Wall with a tea service and con- veyed the congratulations and hearty greetings of the class.

The Rev. H. WALL in returning thanks, said his heart was filled with gladness on receiving those tokens of the affection of the people at Cockhedge. There were times in one's work when one began to feel a little bit despondent, but an occasion like the present gave one new cheer and new hope. It helped one to feel that, after all, there was a bond of unity between pastor and people which always ought to exist and which must exist if the work was to continue to be a success. He trusted the new life upon which he and his wife had entered might be fraught with blessedness and happi- ness and that as they went along together through the days that were before them they might be able by their sympathy, and perhaps at times by their advice and guidance, to lead the people, as they hoped the people might lead them, to better things. He trusted that their fellowship together might be a blessed and happy one. He asked them to extend to his wife, who came amongst them as a stranger, the same sympathy that they had shown to him and then he was sure that their work would be fraught with much good. (Applause.) The Rev. JOHN YONGE said that was a unique gather- ing, as he did not know that Cockhedge Mission had ever before in its history had the pleasure of celebrating the marriage of its pastor. The Wycliffe people wished to join with the Cockhedge congregation in wishing that Mr. and Mrs. Wall might have a, very happy married life. He hoped the friends at Wycliffe and Cockhedge would extend to Mrs. Wall a very sympathetic welcome, and that she might expect to find there those who were perfectly ready to be friends to her, and that she would be a friend to them. He sincerely trusted that, if it were God's will, Mr. and Mrs. Wall might have 44 years as happy, prosperous and useful as by God's blessing he trusted his own and Mrs.Yonge's married life had been. (Applause,)—Mr. Yonge concluded by presenting, on behalf of the Wycliffe friends, a handsome chiming clock and cruet, rerharking that the gifts had been chosen by Mrs. Armitage. They sincerely hoped that Mr. and Mrs. Wall might have a very long and happy m yried life. (Applause). Mr. GEORGE WALLINGTON also offered hearty con- gratulations.

The Rev. EL WALL said it was very kind of the Wycliffe friends to show their sympathy with the work there. He trusted that under new circumstances they might be able to work together even better than in the past, with fewer blemishes and with greater success. (Applause). During the evening an enjoyable musical programme was submitted and refreshments were served.

GREAT BAZAAR AND FETE AT HILL CLIFFE.

The picturesque grounds of "Hill Cliffe," the residence of Mr. Henry Greenall, are at present the scene of a bazaar and fete in which all Warrington is more or less interested. The event represents a great effort to raise the balance of £3,000 required for the Infirmary extension fund and few bazaars have aroused such enthusiasm amongst all sections of the com- munity. The friends of the institution are extremely anxious that the new building shall be opened free from debt,and it is hoped that the generosity with which the appeal has already been responded to will be maintained and that the bazaar will place the success- ful realization of their wishes beyond doubt. The bazaar, which was opened on Wednesday, will be con- tinued to-day and. all who have not already visited it and sampled the numerous attractions should certainly make a point of doing so. Those who are apt to look upon such functions as monotonous will surely change their opinion in this case, for the bazaar is almost unique by reason of the unusual nature of the surround- ings. The park with its fine spreading trees makes a delightful retreat, and nothing could be more pleasant than to spend a few hours at this rural spot away from the grime and bustle of the town. Then, again, there are almost innumerable attractions to entertain the visitor and induce him to part with surplus cash,so that the risk of boredom is nil.

Fine weather is the all-important factor in the success of at fresco functions, and the unpromising out- look of Tuesday gave rise to no little uneasiness. However, Wednesday was more like the June that we once knew, the sun shining brilliantly during the afternoon, while the heat was not at all oppressive. The result was that many inhabitants of the town and district betook themselves to Hill Cliffe and there was a large and representative gathering at flag opening ceremony. Everything augured for the success of the undertaking and visions of thousands of p3unds raised and deficits wiped off, filled the minds of the workers. The stalls were most attractively arranged in a large marquee, a separate one being allotted to each district. They were artistically draped with green and white muslin and were laden with all manner of useful and pretty wares. The following ladies acted as stall- holders :

No one who had not been in an infirmary could realize the amount of good which they did. He happened to spend his first three weeks in India in such an institution, and could fully testify to the skill and attention which was paid to the people on all hands. (Applause.)

THE OPENING CEREMONY.

The bazaar was opened on Wednesday afternoon by the Hon. Ellinor and the Hon. Elisabeth Wilson-Patten. Mr.W. Peter Hylands, J.P., (Chairman of the Infirmary Extension Committee) presided over an excellent atten- dance which included the Mayor and Mayoress and many of the clergy and ministers of the town and district.

The CHAIRMAN, having presented the Hon. Misses Wilson-Patten with souvenir copies of the programme. said that probably the audience had heard so much of the objects of the bazaar that it was hardly necessary for him to dwell at much length upon that point. At the same time he thought it was only right to remind them of the reason for the bazaar, and that was that the Infirmary accommodation at Warrington had been hope- lessly inadequate during the last few years to meet the claims that had been made upon it. They had only forty beds to meet the necessities of a population of close upon 80,000 and the staff of the Infirmary had in the most devoted manner been doing all they could to make those forty beds do the work that was required, until they had been very much handicapped in their labours. The Board of Management were faced with two alternatives—either they would have to leave things as they were or they would have to face a very large scheme which would provide for the requirements for a reasonable number of years. After a most anxious 1 consideration it was decided to adopt the plans, probably with which his hearers were familiar, and the work had been put in hand involving a cost of £15,000. The people of Warrington and the surrounding districts had come forward in the most generous manner and they had already found over .£12,000. (Applause.) He was full of hope that during the four days of the bazaar , the whole of the balance would be cleared off and that the new extension might be opened free of debt. (Applause). in that tent there were an enormous num- ber of pretty and valuable things which the visitors would probably snap up at once if there were no other , consideration but their worth and usefulness. But he ' hoped the consideration of the object they had in view would unloose their generosity to the utmost extent land assist the management in attaining the aim they I had so much at heart. Before asking the Hon. Nfisses I Wilson-Patten to declare the sale open he would like to remind those present that the Hon. Misses Wilson- Patten were the first to put down ^ their =awes for a contribution towards the Infirmary extension. (Applause). The Hon. Miss ELLINOR WILSoN-PATTEN, who was heartily received, said that after what Mr. Rylands had said, she thought nothing remained for her to say I expect that as they were likely to be /mined before they left-.-(laughter)---they must console themselves I with the reflection that it would be in an extremely !good cause, on which all minds were at one. She did not think there could be anyone dissenting from that important undertaking and she had great pleasure in opening the bazaar and hoped that it would be fully prosperous. (Applause). The MAYOR said he had been asked to perform a most pleasing duty, although he thought it might have been I placed in much abler hands. At the same time he felt I it a great honour to be allowed to propose a vote of Ithanks to the Hon. Misses Wilson-Patten for their i kindness in coming such a long distance to open the bazaar. Distance seemed to be no object with them, as long as they were doing something for the good old town of Warrington. (Applause). Their hearts were i constantly with good work n this town, and they would come down even to a humble place like Factory-yard to visit the poor. To-day they were helping forward another splendid object, and lie was very hopeful that the greater part of the .C 3,000 would be raised by the bazaar. If not, they must make another great effort and see that the institution was opened free from debt. They Were reminded by the presence of the Hon. THURSDAY'S PROCEEDS.

Thursday's takings were £ll4, the total for the two days being £1,094 7s. 10d. YESTERDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

There was a great improvement in the weather yester- day, with the result that the attendance at tbe opening ceremony was large.

Mr. dEORGE DAKIN presided, and in asking Mr. William Long to open the bazaar, referred to the generous support which that gentleman had accorded to the institution. If the old saying" He gives twice who gives quickly" were true, then Mr. Long had given twice. He received N'fr. Long's cheque for £l,OOO some days before the meeting at the Town Hall at which the final arrangements for the extension were made. He was sorry they had not been altogether favoured in regard to the weather,but he thought the total takings. £1,094 were satisfactory. (Applause). Mr. LONG said he would pass over the personal element to which the Chairman had alluded. He had simply done what he considered to be his duty in striving to help forward the good work of the Infirmary in Warrington. (Hear, hear). It seemed to him that the growth of the town demanded a larger outlay upon that great philanthropic work—(hear, hear,)—and he was tired of seeing their surgeons and doctors in the , Infirmary waiting and waiting for something to be done. His feelings as he grew older were of increased respect for members of that noble profession. There were men in it who gave not only time but very often their lives to the work and that for a very miserable payment— compared with what commercial men would waet for the time given and the heart and soul with which they I threw themselves into the work. He was thankful to I see that Warrington and the surrounding districts had responded well to the call and hoped the result of the bazaar would be to ease their minds for at least 20 or 30 years, and that they would have no need for further fears for the funds of the institution. If that were so he was sure they would all feel amply repaid for any little sacrifices which they had made. (Hear, hear.) He congratulated them all upon what they had made the two previous days but they must not stop at that and must remember that there was still £l9OO to raise. (Applause.) He had much pleasure in declaring the bazaar open. (Applause.) The Rev. Canon WILLIS proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Long. Mr. Long's donation to the extension was one of she largest and was most generous and handsome even for a rich man.As St. Paul said to the Corinthians, Mr. Long had "provoked many others " to doing good works. He believed Mr. Long's gift was the humblest. Mr. Long was a rnodest,unassuming man and he thought the donation had been given out of gratitude to God for the blessings in business, and out of kind considera- tion for the poor who were unable to provide for them, selves when sick with the proper medical skill. (Applause.) The Rev. FATHER SWARBRECK seconded. It was only due to the managers of the Infirmary and of the fete that he should give the acknowledgments of the body to which he belonged of the good done by the Infirmary. However much they agreed to differ upon certain things they were all united in the common cause and endeavour to increase the usefulness of the institution. (Applause). The vote was heartily accorded. Mr. LONG in reply said that a great incentive to him to help in causes of that kind was that they could meet on common ground. There was neither party nor sect in the matter. He was sick and tired of politics and all the dissentions in so-lalled religions life. There they had no party or sect and they should endeavour to carry that spirit as far as they could into their daily lives. (Applause.) YESTERDAY'S PROCEEDS.
