Municipalization of the Drink Trade.

Sir,—Tt would appear by the letter of Mr. Stanley Lord in " the Voice of Labour " that the Labour ' Party or rather the Socialistic portion thereof, are the only "benefactors of mankind " who are prepared to deal (?) with the drink trade. His outspoken remarks with reference to the "temperance fanatics "are some- what appropriate. We do assume' we even assert, "that strong drink is an unmixed (and mixed) evil, alcohol an Invention of the Devil and the public- house an ante-room to Hades." This just falls in line with my own particular fanatical ideas. Will Mr. Lord kindly prove anything to the contrary? Whom shall we include in "most of the world's greatest men" who have indulged in "stimulants ? " and who the " sweetest singers who have sang in praise of Bacchus ?" I have no doubt they have done. There are those who will sing any kind of song if well paid and unfortunately there are those who can only sing to the acoompaniment of the pint mug rattle on a Saturday or Sunday night. Your correspondent states that" without a doubt the great nations of the earth are all heavy drinkers." This statement may perhaps in some degree be tree. But will he bring forward any- thine. to prove that the drink has, in any degree, con- tributed to the greatness of any nation? To do this he must have higher authority than Lord Roberts, Lord Charles Beresford, Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman and the present Government (Labour and Socialists included). Has Christian England contributed to the " greatness " of the heathen lands by the liquor trade consignments she is guilty of introducing to the natives ? Ask any of our Missionaries and their answer will be that England's " Bottled Gospel "is the greatest stumbling block in the path of progress and civilisation. I have no desire at this particular juncture to deal with the" wickedness" and "foolishness , ' of "moderation." The majority of drunkards' graves are filled by those who were once of the '• moderation Elect." After criticising prohibitive measures and admitting and deploring at the same time. the" evils of over-indulgeixes," Mr. Lord goes on to say "The only sensible reniedy so far propounded is the Socialist one of municipalization. Thee how strange, that when the Scottish Local Veto Bill came on for second reading in the House of Commons every Labour member present voted in its favour and not one of their gentle voices uttered a cry in the interests of the socialistic element of their con- stituency. I wonder if they feared a shower of " West Ham revelations "would damp their ardour at Westminster if they dared to " broach the subject " so dear to their hearts in January 1906. It also appears by the large majorities at numerous Trades Union meetings that the socialist proposal is a sad failure. Surely the Municipal enterprise expounded by Mr. Lord is a unique one. We shoulo have Beautiful Warrington all at once. The pnblic house would be the "working man's club," under his ideal imaginary condi- tion. "Very few establishments would be needed, one in every district would be sufficient 'with a. number of off-licence places for the supply of supper beer, etc." We may, of course, assume that the greed and avarice of the socialist would in no way disqualify him from becoming an "off-license " holder. Your correspondent has indeed painted a true picture of the scene as he says " there is no doubt it would be profitable," the Socialistic portion of the community undoubtedly " wou/d, benefit." But before he endeavours to "enlarge upon this idea "will be good enough to prove how it would, under these imaginary conditions, "be impos3ible to get drunk." The reason why Mr. Lord asks will , the 1.0.G.T. join the fray ? "I presume arises from the fact that we are among his strongest opponents on the question of municipalization. In con- clusion may I respectfully inform my friends of the Independent Labour Party that I and the organization am proud to be a member of will never become con- taminated with the enemy we have pledged ourselves to oppose. Had he not have brought in the 1.0.G.T. I should have ignored his contribution, which I feel sure will have in the eyes of your readers, done nothing to injure the temperance cause, and less to advance the Socialistic scheme owing to the glaring spirit of greed and avarice which lies at the foundation of such a rabid proposal. There ars now such a number of " cures ' ? belauded for this gigantic evil that the public are at a loss to know which is the "genuine article." My advice te your readers is do not be led away with the high-sounding phrases of Municipalize- tion,Disinterested Management or Public House Trusts, but stick to the old "tried and true "path of personal influence and Prohibition for the people by the people— the principle adopted by your forefathers. It is most gratifying to see the growth of temperance sentiment and I hope the day is not far distant when the Christian Church will arise from her lethargy and speak out with , no ungertain voice in favour of England's freedom ; from the slavery of drinlidom. Yours &c., J. R. GRIFFITHS, Moore, June 19th, 1907.

HISTORY OF THE INSTITUTION.

The following history of the institution, taken from the bazaar handbook, will doubtless be read with interest: At a meeting held on the 15th of January, 1810, nearly a century ago, presided over by Mr. John Blackburn, M.P., it was resolved" Mat a Dispensary for the relief of the sick poor in this town and neighbourhood should, if possible, be established," and a committee was appointed to carr3r. the object into effect. On the list of the members of this committee are to be found names which are still represented in the town's front ranks by the descendants of the leaders of 1810—Blackburne, Crosfield, Greenall, Litton, Lyon, Parr, Patten, Pickmere, and Rylands. The Dis- pensary was opened for the admission of patients on the 12th of April, 1810, in a building in Market-street rented by the committee. The first officials were Mr. John Blackburn, M.P., president; Mr. Peter Patten, M.P., vice-president; Mr. G. Crosfield, treasurer; and Mr. Glazebrook, secretary; whilst Dr. Kendrick and Dr. Turner were appointed physicians to the institution. In 1819 the erection of the first Dispensary was com- pleted on a site near the present Roman Catholic Church (St. Mary's) in Buttermarket-street, and here the great and noble work was continued for many years.l In 1840 the committee erected an isolation hospital in Orford-street, which was called the "[louse of Recovery," but all this time the town was without any infirmary accommodation for the treatment of accidents. In 1870 Mr. Thomas Parr bequeathed £l,OOO towards the erection of an infirmary, and in 1871 Mr. James Hatton gave £5,000, followed by a further £5,000 in 1873, towards the same object. In the last-named year the Town Council made a free grant of a plot of land in Bank Park, on which the present Infirmary and Dis- pensary Building was erected. The original building, erected in 1876, cost 43,000, and provided accommoda- tion for 23 beds. In 1890, this accommodation was increased to 40 beds, together with a new dispensary and operating theatre, at a further cost of £5,500. Meantime the population of the town and neighbourhood continued to grow rapidly, and last year it was announced that, in the opinion of the Board of Management, "an immediate and substantial extension of the accommodation was a matter of urgent necessity," and plans were adopted whereby the accommodation of the Infirmary will be increased to a total of 100 beds, at an estimated cost of £15,000. The appeal for the necessary funds to carry out this large and important extension met with a ready and splendid response from all classes of the com- munity, and it is hoped that the result of the present Bazaar and Fete may go far towards providing, the balance of £3,000 which is still required. The need is recognised by all. Whilst 1886 saw 20 operations, with 162 in-patients altogether, in 1906 there were 607 in-patients and 307 operations. The new home-patients treated during 1906 numbered 1,411, and 3,652 new out-patients were treated at the Dispensary. The accidents and emergencies treated in the accident room numbered 3,769. The total number of cases treated in the Dispensary last year was 8,862. The sphere served by the Institution • extends to ten miles from Warrington, so that town and country alike are under obligation to contribute to it. Its moumful history of accident and disease, illumined by the bright story of cure and recovery, albeit shadowed ever and anon by the gloom of death, is spread before our eyes. The pitiful appeal of the sick and the broken goes to our hearts. Lot us see to it that nothing hinders the work of our blessed" House of Recovery."

THE MARRIED STATE. Extraordinary Allegations Against a Warrington Man.

At Warrington Police Court, yesterday, Bober Williams, 42, Cartwright-street. was charged with pert sistent cruelty to his wife, Ann Williams, 52, Baxter- street, who asked for a separation. Mr. Browne, for the complainant, stated that the parties were married at St. Paul's Church. Warrington, on August 3rd, 1884. Defendant was a roller at Monks's tube works and when in regular employment he could earn 525. per week. Lately, however2his standing wages , were 355. Te cruelty commenced six months after the marriage. Eight years ago defendant stabbed his wife on the right 4ido of the face with a table knife, and the mark was still to be seen. He had given her black eyes,and on one occasion when there were friends in the honse he brought in a shovelful of slack and, instead of putting it on the fire, as they all thought he was going to do, be poured it over complainant's head. He had also run her out of the house, and the son would say that defendant waited for her behind the door with a shovelful of red-hot cinders. Those, said Mr. Browne, were only a few of the details. Mr. Steel, for the defendant, asked that an order should be made without going into the 'details. 'rho bench thereupon granted a separation order, carrying 15s. per week, complainant to have the custody of the four children, whose ages range from 18 to 9 years.

MINISTER'S CRYP rlc POSTER.

The Rev. J. 0. Stalberg, minister of the Bunyan Baptist Church, Kingston-on-Thames, has issued a remarkable pictorial poster announcing a midsummer morning service next Sunday. The only clue forth- coming as to the rev, gentleman's address is in a num- ber of marks of interrogation to be seen amidst the rays of what is apparently a limelight lantern. The ministe has designed and issued the poster to give the memberr of his flock the opportunity of exercising their menta. powers in order to explain this pictorial conundrura.

LOCAL WEDDINGS. • Houghton—Collin. There was a large congregation at St. Paul's Church on Thursday to witness the marriage of Mr. Harold Beswick Houghton, son of Mr. James Houghton, Warrington, and Miss Mary Collin daughter of the late Mr. Robert Wo)d Collin, builder, Warringtos. The officiating clergy were the Revs. W. Bracecamp and E. E. Peters. The bride was prettily attired in a dress of silk chiffon taffeta, trimmed with tucked chiffon and old Honiton point lace, with a leghorn hat trimmed with silk merveilleux ribbon. She carried a shower bouquet of roses and white heather. The. bridesmaid, Miss Frances Parkinson, niece of the bride, wore a dress of white muslin and hat to match, trimmed with roses, and carried a bouquet of sweet peas. Mr. James Houghton, jun., brother of the bridegroom, discharged the duties of best man. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., was at the organ and con- tributed several wedding marches, whilst the hymns "The voice that breathed o'er Eden" and "0 perfect love" were sung. The reception was held at Oak Bank, Grappenhall-road, Stockton Heath,the residence of the bride, after which the newly-married couple left for North Wales for the honeymoon,the bride travelling in a blue serge tailor-made costume with hat to match. Amongst the many presents received were a silver rose bowl, vase, cream jug and sugar basin from the members of the Junior Conservative Association, with which Mr. Houghton is actively identified. Broomfield—Evans. There was a pretty wedding at St. Barnabas' Church on Thursday afternoon, the contracting parties being Miss Edith Annie Evans, eldest daughter of Mr. T. Evans, Folly-lane, organist of the Church, and Mr. Harry Broomfield,a member of the choir,and eldeat son of Mr. Richard Broomfield, Prieetley-stroot Conaider- able interest was taken in the event and a large con- gregation assembled. The Vicar (Rev. J. H. G.- Hollingworth) officiated. The bride, who was given away by her father, was charmingly attired in crepe-de-chine, trimmed with silk and lace, and crinoline hat trimmed with orange blossom and jesamine. She carried a lovely bouquet of orange blossom and roses. She was attended by four bridesmaids Misses Elsie A., Cissie and Eva Evan (sisters of the bride), and Maggie Broomfield (sister of the bridegroom) who were very tastefully attired and carried beautiful bouquets. Mr. E. Iddon acted as best man. Mr. T. Reeves presided at the organ and played festal selections, including Ifendelssohn's " Wedding March." The reception was held at the house of the bride's father, a large number of guests attending. The happy couple were the recipients of manylhandsome presents.

COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER. NTOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next 111 GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER will be holden at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, in and for the said County on MONDAY, the Ist day of July, 1907, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Broxton, Chester Castle, Dares- bury, Eddisbury, Nantwich, Runcorn, Winsford, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-mentioned Divisions, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the Opening of the Court on MONDAY, the Ist of July, 1907, at Half- past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COURT will be adjourned to the COURT HOUSE, KNUTSFORD, on WEDNESDAY, the 3rd day of July, 1907, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Altrincham, Bucklow, Dukinfield„ Middlewich, Sandbach, Congleton, Northwich, Prost- bury, and Stockport, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from such Divisions will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford, at the Opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 3rd day of July next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. Chester, 12th June, 1907.
